Morality,
between us.

We argue that human morality is an intersubjective framework: its standards are constituted by what can be justified to those affected, with personal judgment and accountability.

If morality is made between people, why isn’t anything allowed?

Start with objectivity. Follow the reasons. Put them under pressure in real cases.

    MORARG · The reading version

    Morality, between us.

    We argue that human morality is an intersubjective framework: its standards are constituted by what can be justified to those affected, with personal judgment and accountability.

    The claim concerns what makes a human moral standard valid. We defend affected interests, agency and reciprocity as its basis, then test that account against serious objections and real cases.

    Collect values, compare responses and keep working notes. Use the controls without 3D.

    Evidence, argument and values: how to read this
    Definition
    A distinction that makes the disagreement precise.
    Argument
    An authored philosophical argument, open to challenge.
    Evidence
    A documented fact or finding, within its stated limits.
    Commitment
    A normative premise defended with reasons, not a laboratory result.
    Provisional
    A conclusion whose remaining burden is identified.

    Research tests factual claims; reasons connect facts and principles to decisions. The argument does not treat a study as a proof of metaethysics. Choice feedback specifies a claim to repair or a disagreement to work through. Branches adapt to selected answers; dependent responses need reconfirmation if their context changes. Selected-claim checks show the paired answers and permit a recorded explanation. Values record priorities, not evidence weights.

    00 · Good, Bad and Objective?

    When would a judgment of “good” be objective?

    We argue that human morality is an intersubjective framework: its standards are constituted by what can be justified to those affected, with personal judgment and accountability.

    Human evaluation needs a defended standard; conviction and vocabulary cannot supply its authority.

    Three different claims Definition

    A consequence can occur regardless of what we wish. A judgment can be tested publicly and impartially. A moral truth can be said to hold independently of our evaluative approval. These are different senses of objectivity. A realist may ground rightness in human needs while denying that our approval makes it right; realism need not imagine a morality with no humans in it.

    • Moral RealismPrimary philosophy · The Philosophical Review 95(2), 163–207; especially pp. 164–176 and 189–198
      What this source supports

      A naturalist, relational account that permits criticism of actual approval.

      Access: Full primary article retrieved; selected sections on objectivity, informed interests and social rationality inspected in pass002

      Limits: Selected-section review, not an exhaustive assessment. The natural basis of interests must not be confused with approval creating the truth.

    • A Darwinian Dilemma for Realist Theories of ValuePrimary philosophy · Philosophical Studies 127, 109–166; §§1–3, 5–7; pass003 adds §5, pp. 123–124 and §10, pp. 152–155
      What this source supports

      Challenges evolutionary reliability and sketches how actual evaluative error can survive an account grounded in a wider system of attitudes.

      Access: Full primary article retrieved; selected sections inspected in pass002; reflective-equilibrium and concluding antirealist passages inspected in pass003

      Limits: Evolutionary causes alone do not refute realism. The concluding sketch is not a full defence of one constructivism and does not itself establish MORARG’s equal-standing premise.

    Good and bad

    Good for what, and for whom? Definition

    “Good” and “bad” evaluate, but they do different work in different claims. Make the standard visible before treating the judgment as a description of reality or a report of taste.

    1. Good for a purpose

      A tool may perform its task well. That can be tested, even by someone who rejects the task. An effective instrument of coercion need not be morally good. Success at an aim does not justify the aim.

    2. Good for someone

      A policy may improve a person’s health, security or opportunities. Specify whose welfare, over what time and at what cost. Facts can defeat a person’s current belief about what benefits them; benefiting one person may burden another.

    3. Morally good

      Now the question is which ends and burdens deserve support, whose claims count and what is owed. Geach’s account connects evaluation with the kind of thing assessed; it does not make goodness depend on the speaker’s approval. Moore offers a rival account of goodness. Neither settles this moral question by a dictionary.

    • Good and EvilPrimary philosophy: article transcription · Analysis 17(2), 33–42; transcription paragraphs 1–3, 8–12 and 15–16
      What this source supports

      Argues that good and bad are logically attributive, and rejects reducing their descriptive use to the speaker’s commendation or tastes.

      Access: Full article transcription available; selected passages inspected in pass004; publisher-indexed title, DOI and pagination checked, while direct publisher retrieval failed

      Limits: Geach’s attributive thesis is contested. It does not entail subjectivism or MORARG’s constitutive thesis; his transition to moral assessment and motivation requires further argument. The transcription header gives 32–42; publisher metadata gives 33–42.

      What this source supports

      Distinguishes a verbal definition from inquiry into goodness and argues against treating a substantive account of the good as a result of word meaning.

      Access: Full book transcription available at Project Gutenberg; selected opening passages inspected in pass004, not the whole book

      Limits: Moore’s indefinability thesis is his philosophical position, not an established refutation of every naturalist identity theory. MORARG uses it as a serious countermodel to the proposed semantic shortcut.

    Good and bad

    Subjective judgment, shared justification Argument

    A judgment is made by a subject, from a standpoint. That does not mean its truth is whatever that person approves. A realist can agree that judging is a human activity while denying approval makes the judgment true. Our stronger claim needs its own argument.

    1. Begin with what matters to lives

      Pain, dependence, agency and aspiration give evaluation its human stakes. They are not erased by a spectator’s dislike. Hume’s sentimental route also distinguishes moral assessment from the judge’s immediate advantage.

    2. Add the moral premise openly

      When a judgment claims authority over someone else, their comparable interests deserve standing in its justification. My satisfaction alone cannot answer their serious loss. This premise is defended through reciprocal claims; it is not contained in the word “good”.

    3. Make the standard intersubjective

      We propose that a defensible relation among affected people constitutes interpersonal validity. Evidence constrains our judgments, other people can correct them, and personal responsibility remains. This is a substantive account of human morality, stronger than individual preference and still challenged by realism.

    • A Treatise of Human Nature, Book IIIPrimary philosophy · T 3.1.1.27; T 3.1.2.1–4, SBN 470–472; T 3.3.1.15–18, SBN 581–584
      What this source supports

      Defends a sentimental account while distinguishing moral approval from immediate private advantage and describing correction toward a steadier general standpoint.

      Access: Selected primary passages inspected in pass001; pass004 directly inspects sentiment, private-interest distinctions and the general standpoint in Hume Texts Online

      Limits: An argued philosophical account, not empirical confirmation or a complete interpretation of Hume. The general standpoint is a precedent for correction, not proof of MORARG’s equal-standing or constitutive-authority premises; the is–ought passage alone does not refute naturalism.

      What this source supports

      Connects equal normative authority with reciprocal justification and explicitly states that constructed norms depend on prior normativity of principles and standing.

      Access: Berkeley-hosted full manuscript available; selected grounding passages inspected in pass004; publisher-indexed PDF verified publication metadata but direct publisher retrieval failed

      Limits: Focuses on basic legal rights of recognised members; the membership question is bracketed. Not a premise-free derivation, proof of all moral constructivism or independent review of every rival rights theory.

    Good and bad

    Being sure is not what makes it so Argument

    Feeling a judgment as obvious tells us about conviction. It may reflect insight, experience or habit; certainty alone cannot decide which. Equally, having an emotional response does not discredit a judgment. The reasons and the people affected still need to be heard.

    1. Keep conviction and warrant apart

      Two people can be equally certain of incompatible judgments. Their certainty does not make both judgments true, nor show that neither has a better case. Ask what supports each claim.

    2. Correct the standpoint

      Hume describes judging from a steadier, more general view rather than letting proximity or private advantage determine praise. Here that becomes a practical discipline: hear affected people, test facts, compare alternatives and allow challenge.

    3. Defend the conclusion

      A humane judgment can be firm and revisable. We favour standards people can answer for to one another; a realist may understand the same correction as discovering independent truth. The difference concerns their ground, not how confident their supporters sound.

    The case on offer Commitment

    Our claim is stronger than recommending discussion. A human moral standard is valid in virtue of being defensible to those affected under reciprocal, informed and unforced consideration of their claims. Those justificatory relations constitute the standard; actual approval does not. People still interpret and decide. Equal standing, agency and constraints on imposed harm are the moral starting points we will defend, rather than pretend to derive from observations alone.

    • Contractualism and UtilitarianismPrimary philosophy · In Utilitarianism and Beyond, pp. 103–128; indexed opening discussion of justification and moral theory
      What this source supports

      A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

      Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    • A Treatise of Human Nature, Book IIIPrimary philosophy · T 3.1.1.27; T 3.1.2.1–4, SBN 470–472; T 3.3.1.15–18, SBN 581–584

      Limits: An argued philosophical account, not empirical confirmation or a complete interpretation of Hume. The general standpoint is a precedent for correction, not proof of MORARG’s equal-standing or constitutive-authority premises; the is–ought passage alone does not refute naturalism.

    What would make a demand authoritative? Argument

    Knowing a beneficial action is not the same as having authority to demand it. Someone wronged can be owed an answer or repair. We argue that a properly justified relation between people constitutes that interpersonal authority. This gives standing, agency and correction a common explanation. A realist can accept all these relations while grounding their authority in independent reasons. The case therefore needs a defended grounding step; useful discussion or a shared verdict cannot settle it. Construction can also let us discover what a valid relation requires.

    • Authority and Second-Personal Reasons for ActingPrimary philosophy: author manuscript · Reasons for Action, chapter 7, pp. 134–154; accessed manuscript pp. 1–7 and 13–16
      What this source supports

      Distinguishes reasons for beneficial compliance from a person’s standing to demand compliance and hold another accountable.

      Access: Yale-hosted full manuscript available; selected passages on authority, accountability and expertise inspected in pass004; Cambridge publication record verified

      Limits: The interdefinition of valid demand and authority does not establish everyone’s standing or prove antirealism. The 2006 book and 2013 reprint were not reviewed in full.

    Take a position

    1. When you say morality is objective, which claim matters most to you?

      Separate what happens, how a judgment is tested, and what makes it morally true. None of these selections is an error.

      Argument

      Independence from approval differs from independence from human interests.

      • Moral RealismPrimary philosophy · The Philosophical Review 95(2), 163–207; especially pp. 164–176 and 189–198
        What this source supports

        A naturalist, relational account that permits criticism of actual approval.

        Access: Full primary article retrieved; selected sections on objectivity, informed interests and social rationality inspected in pass002

        Limits: Selected-section review, not an exhaustive assessment. The natural basis of interests must not be confused with approval creating the truth.

      Moral truth is not made true by our approval.

      This is the central realist challenge. It can allow moral facts to concern human needs and relationships; it need not put morality outside human life.

      contested

      Moral judgment can be publicly tested beyond my preference.

      This is the objectivity the proposed practice aims to earn. It leaves open whether its standards are discovered or constructed.

      Consequences occur regardless of what I want.

      That gives moral reasoning factual constraints. Which consequences count as reasons, and how strongly, is a further question.

      Holding the distinction open avoids resolving a substantive debate by a definition.

    2. Good and bad

      A proposed service is efficient and benefits most users, but places a serious burden on a few. What should “good” require us to examine?

      Hypothetical case: the evidence about efficiency is accepted. The size of the benefit, the burden, feasible alternatives and the people’s claims still need assessment. More than one question matters; choose your first priority.

      Argument

      Geach challenges treating every use of “good” as one free-standing property or as the speaker’s commendation. His account is an argued position, not a definition that proves subjectivism.

      • Good and EvilPrimary philosophy: article transcription · Analysis 17(2), 33–42; transcription paragraphs 1–3, 8–12 and 15–16
        What this source supports

        Argues that good and bad are logically attributive, and rejects reducing their descriptive use to the speaker’s commendation or tastes.

        Access: Full article transcription available; selected passages inspected in pass004; publisher-indexed title, DOI and pagination checked, while direct publisher retrieval failed

        Limits: Geach’s attributive thesis is contested. It does not entail subjectivism or MORARG’s constitutive thesis; his transition to moral assessment and motivation requires further argument. The transcription header gives 32–42; publisher metadata gives 33–42.

      Clarify the purpose before deciding whether the service performs it well.

      This tests instrumental success. It still leaves whether that purpose and the chosen means deserve support; a harmful aim can be pursued efficiently.

      Examine what the benefits and burdens mean for each person’s welfare.

      This makes “good for whom” concrete. Welfare can be misjudged, and someone’s present preference is not the whole account of their interests. A further judgment must compare competing claims.

      Ask whether the burdens can be justified to those who bear them.

      This is the proposed moral test. It gives the minority standing without assuming every complaint is decisive. Explain severity, alternatives and the comparison of claims.

      Examine whether the service’s aim itself deserves moral support.

      A good result relative to a selected aim need not vindicate that aim. State the values by which you assess it and answer the affected people’s competing claims.

    3. Good and bad

      Every moral judgment is made from a person’s standpoint. Which further claim about its validity would you defend?

      “Subjective” can mean involving a subject who judges, or having truth fixed by that subject’s approval. The first does not entail the second. Choose a substantive account; each needs reasons beyond the existence of a judge.

      Argument

      Our positive bridge is normative: comparable affected interests deserve a place in justifying a demand that governs them. The vocabulary of evaluation does not supply that premise.

      • The Justification of Basic Rights: A Discourse-Theoretical ApproachPrimary philosophy: author manuscript · Netherlands Journal of Legal Philosophy 45(3), 7–28; manuscript §§1–2, pp. 1–9; especially p. 8
        What this source supports

        Connects equal normative authority with reciprocal justification and explicitly states that constructed norms depend on prior normativity of principles and standing.

        Access: Berkeley-hosted full manuscript available; selected grounding passages inspected in pass004; publisher-indexed PDF verified publication metadata but direct publisher retrieval failed

        Limits: Focuses on basic legal rights of recognised members; the membership question is bracketed. Not a premise-free derivation, proof of all moral constructivism or independent review of every rival rights theory.

        What this source supports

        Limits: These are 2009 lectures, not the revised 2014 book. Selected passages do not provide a complete theory of conflicting claims or settle the prior-reasons objection.

      A standard’s interpersonal validity is constituted by justification to those affected.

      This is our preferred account: their claims have standing, so your approval alone cannot settle what they are owed. It must defend equal standing and why adequate justification is sufficient, rather than merely a way to discover the answer.

      contested

      Human judgments can be made true by facts or reasons independent of approval.

      A serious realist view. Railton’s informed-interest account can be human and relational while independent of present endorsement. It needs a defended account of the relevant facts or reasons and their authority; the presence of a judge does not refute it.

      contested

      Moral validity is relative to each judging person’s evaluative attitudes.

      That is an individual subjectivist proposal, not a consequence of the word “judgment”. Explain which attitudes count, how mistaken beliefs are corrected and what authority one person’s standard has when another bears its cost.

      contested

      The fact that people judge leaves the truth-making question open.

      This preserves the distinction without answering the deeper dispute. To move further, compare the explanations of authority, error, correction and conflicting claims.

        Limits: Moore’s indefinability thesis is his philosophical position, not an established refutation of every naturalist identity theory. MORARG uses it as a serious countermodel to the proposed semantic shortcut.

        What this source supports

    4. Good and bad

      You feel certain that a rule is good; a person burdened by it offers a reasoned objection. How will you test your judgment?

      The objection may or may not succeed. Neither your confidence nor the other person’s emotion determines the answer. Assume time permits a serious review before acting.

      Argument

      In Hume’s account, a general standpoint corrects the influence of proximity and private advantage. This is a precedent for disciplined human evaluation, not a proof of MORARG’s grounding thesis.

      • A Treatise of Human Nature, Book IIIPrimary philosophy · T 3.1.1.27; T 3.1.2.1–4, SBN 470–472; T 3.3.1.15–18, SBN 581–584
        What this source supports

        Defends a sentimental account while distinguishing moral approval from immediate private advantage and describing correction toward a steadier general standpoint.

        Access: Selected primary passages inspected in pass001; pass004 directly inspects sentiment, private-interest distinctions and the general standpoint in Hume Texts Online

        Limits: An argued philosophical account, not empirical confirmation or a complete interpretation of Hume. The general standpoint is a precedent for correction, not proof of MORARG’s equal-standing or constitutive-authority premises; the is–ought passage alone does not refute naturalism.

      Examine their account of the burden and whether I missed an alternative.

      You use another standpoint to test facts and assumptions. Hearing the complaint gives it a fair assessment, not an automatic victory. The eventual conclusion needs the comparison of reasons.

    5. A reliable study predicts that a policy reduces injuries. What else does your moral conclusion need?

      Assume the prediction is sound. The remaining question is why preventing those injuries justifies this policy, given its other costs.

      An account of why the harms matter and how other burdens compare.

      This supplies an explicit normative bridge. You may defend it through rights, welfare, reciprocity or another account.

      A defended naturalist account connecting those facts to rightness.

      A serious realist route. The identification must be argued for; it is not supplied merely by measuring an outcome.

    6. What role could a shared procedure play in your view?

      Two people may use the same careful method while disagreeing about why its conclusions have authority.

      It helps us discover moral truths that do not depend on approval.

      Realism can endorse public criticism and personal judgment. This experience must address that explanation rather than count your participation as conversion.

    7. Conditional follow-up. Shown when “What role could a shared procedure play in your view?” → “Under justified constraints, it helps constitute our moral standards.” or “I doubt a shared procedure can escape domination or circularity.”. In the interactive version, a changed trigger requires reconsidering dependent responses.

      Two perfectly informed groups reason coherently, but one protects equal standing and the other accepts hereditary domination. Can the procedure distinguish them, and which premise does that work?

      This tests the construction’s starting conditions. Neither factual accuracy nor internal consistency settles whose claims deserve weight. The proposed account must defend its reciprocal starting point, rather than treating every coherent agreement as valid.

      The dominated person’s comparable claim must count; coherence cannot cancel it.

      This is the account’s substantive commitment. Defend why another person’s lower status is not itself a reason to discount the very complaint against that status. It is not a deduction from coherence alone.

    Three values to consider

    Clarity

    Say which objectivity you mean.

    Keep physical consequences, public methods and truth independent of approval distinct. A dispute about one should not be won by silently switching to another.

    Truth beyond us

    What makes a judgment true?

    Carry the realist question forward: could a whole community be mistaken, and what would make it so? Selecting this concern is compatible with valuing shared inquiry.

    Public answerability

    Let others examine the reasons.

    A claim governing shared lives should expose its evidence, premises and remaining burdens to challenge. This is a proposed standard of justification, with its own defence to make.

    01 · Evolved Fairness?

    From a reaction to a reason

    A monkey refusing a lesser reward is observable. What that reaction means—and whether it gives us a moral obligation—requires further argument.

    A capacity may have an evolutionary history without settling what justice requires.

    A famous experiment Evidence

    In 2003 researchers reported that capuchin monkeys sometimes rejected a less desirable reward when another received a better one for a similar exchange. The observation inspired a hypothesis about evolutionary roots of fairness. Refusing food, however, does not by itself tell us whether an animal judged an outcome unjust.

    • Monkeys reject unequal payPrimary empirical research · Nature 425, 297–299; experiment and controls
      What this source supports

      Reported capuchin refusals under unequal reward conditions.

      Access: Primary article PDF indexed and selected abstract and methods inspected

      Limits: The interpretation as inequity aversion is contested by later reanalysis and alternative explanations.

    What the newer evidence adds Evidence

    A 2024 reanalysis of 23 accept/reject studies across 18 species found no statistically clear pooled inequity effect in that paradigm. A 2025 capuchin group study and a 2026 chimpanzee study report responses sensitive to food and social context; another 2026 four-species study found no increased refusals when inequality occurred in 25% or 50% of trials. The pooled analysis also found increased refusals when better food was salient, supporting a disappointment explanation; task design still matters. A null result in one task does not rule out every capacity.

    • No evidence for inequity aversion in non-human animals: a meta-analysis of accept/reject paradigmsPrimary empirical research · Proceedings of the Royal Society B 291, 20241452; individual-participant data, 23 studies and 18 species
      What this source supports

      Reports no statistically clear pooled evidence of inequity aversion in studied accept/reject tasks.

      Access: Indexed primary full text and abstract inspected; PMC page returned an access challenge on this pass

      Limits: A null result is not proof of no effect or no capacity in all paradigms; samples and available data limit inference.

    • Capuchin monkeys’ responses to inequity in a group contextPrimary empirical research · Animal Behaviour 226, 123257; abstract and indexed discussion excerpts
      What this source supports

      Reports refusals of low-value rewards under disadvantageous inequity, with separate contrast and advantageous-inequity responses in a group setting.

      Access: Publisher abstract and selected indexed snippets inspected; full paper not reviewed

      Limits: Food value and contrast affect interpretation; number of trades did not depend on the partner’s reward. No independent reanalysis.

    • Chimpanzees’ responses to inequity in a group context are influenced by food quality and their relationships with the group members presentPrimary empirical research · Proceedings of the Royal Society B 293, 20260397; selected §§2–4, design, measures and discussion
      What this source supports

      Reports context-sensitive responses in a group of chimpanzees.

      Access: Pass003: selected methods, results and discussion inspected in publisher-provided full text hosted on ResearchGate; no data reanalysis

      Limits: Voluntary participation in six captive groups; affiliation is not experimentally assigned. Advantageous refusals were too rare for statistical analysis. These measures do not establish a justice concept.

    • No Response to Inconsistently Experienced Inequity in Four Primate SpeciesPrimary empirical research · American Journal of Primatology; DOI 10.1002/ajp.70196; abstract
      What this source supports

      At 25% or 50% unequal trials the studied primates did not increase refusals relative to controls.

      Access: Publisher and PubMed primary-study abstracts inspected; full text not reviewed

      Limits: Null refusal effects do not establish absence of noticing or other responses; task frequency and measures matter.

    Evidence that pushes back Evidence

    A 2025 bonobo study compared human and machine reward delivery. Its results challenge the idea that refusals are merely disappointment with a human experimenter. The small captive sample and partly matching pattern still limit the conclusion. This is positive evidence to assess, not something a pooled null lets us ignore. Neither pattern establishes a universal moral rule.

    • Bonobos respond aversively to unequal reward distributionsPrimary empirical research · Proceedings of the Royal Society B 292, 20242873; §§2–4, subjects, controls, results and limitations
      What this source supports

      Two bonobo studies report unequal-reward responses; a human/machine manipulation challenges the social-disappointment explanation.

      Access: Primary full-text indexed methods/results/discussion inspected in pass003; no independent reanalysis

      Limits: Six newly tested bonobos in one group; one analysis pooled an older sample. Study 2 was only partly consistent with inequity aversion; partner variation and task constraints limit generalisation.

    What about human beginnings? Evidence

    Human development needs its own evidence. A large 2025 hill-task replication found no above-chance preference for helpers among 567 included infants aged 5.5–10.5 months. A 2026 study using looking measures at 14–15 months found expectations about particular caring relationships. Different ages and measures answer different questions. Neither finding licenses “babies have no social capacities” or “humans are born knowing universal justice”.

    • Infants’ Social Evaluation of Helpers and Hinderers: A Large-Scale, Multi-Lab, Coordinated Replication StudyPrimary empirical research · Developmental Science 28, e13581; abstract; correction DOI 10.1111/desc.70029
      What this source supports

      A large coordinated hill-task replication found no above-chance helper preference.

      Access: Primary abstract and indexed methods inspected; published correction inspected by Terra; no independent reanalysis

      Limits: 567 included infants aged 5.5–10.5 months; this task is not a test of all moral capacities. The 2025 correction changes an exploratory analysis, with conclusions unchanged.

    • Infants use helping to infer the existence and strength of caring relationshipsPrimary empirical research · PNAS 123, e2531771123; abstract, four preregistered experiments
      What this source supports

      Looking-time findings in 14–15-month-olds suggest relationship-specific expectations about helping.

      Access: Primary abstract and selected indexed methods/results inspected; full independent assessment not completed

      Limits: An indirect measure at a different age, not a direct replication of the hill-choice task; it establishes neither innateness nor moral authority.

    A feeling is a starting point Argument

    Sensitivity to outcomes or expectations may be among the capacities from which social practices develop; these experiments do not establish an innate, fixed sense of justice. To argue that a distribution is unjust, we must also ask whose interests count, what differences matter and how burdens are justified. An evolutionary explanation neither proves nor disproves moral realism. This exhibit defends moral standards constituted through reasons people can contest and share, rather than claiming those standards follow automatically from biology.

    • No evidence for inequity aversion in non-human animals: a meta-analysis of accept/reject paradigmsPrimary empirical research · Proceedings of the Royal Society B 291, 20241452; individual-participant data, 23 studies and 18 species
      What this source supports

      Reports no statistically clear pooled evidence of inequity aversion in studied accept/reject tasks.

      Access: Indexed primary full text and abstract inspected; PMC page returned an access challenge on this pass

      Limits: A null result is not proof of no effect or no capacity in all paradigms; samples and available data limit inference.

    • Capuchin monkeys’ responses to inequity in a group contextPrimary empirical research · Animal Behaviour 226, 123257; abstract and indexed discussion excerpts
      What this source supports

    Take a position

    1. What can a refusal of a lesser reward tell us?

      The monkey rejects food after an exchange in a particular experimental context.

      A measurable response that may have several explanations.

      That keeps the behaviour distinct from its interpretation. Social comparison, expectation and reward value can require different controls.

      reflection

      • Monkeys reject unequal payPrimary empirical research · Nature 425, 297–299; experiment and controls
        What this source supports

        Reported capuchin refusals under unequal reward conditions.

        Access: Primary article PDF indexed and selected abstract and methods inspected

        Limits: The interpretation as inequity aversion is contested by later reanalysis and alternative explanations.

      • No evidence for inequity aversion in non-human animals: a meta-analysis of accept/reject paradigmsPrimary empirical research · Proceedings of the Royal Society B 291, 20241452; individual-participant data, 23 studies and 18 species
        What this source supports

        Reports no statistically clear pooled evidence of inequity aversion in studied accept/reject tasks.

        Access: Indexed primary full text and abstract inspected; PMC page returned an access challenge on this pass

        Limits: A null result is not proof of no effect or no capacity in all paradigms; samples and available data limit inference.

      It proves the animal possesses the same moral concept of justice as a human.

      A refusal alone cannot identify the animal’s concept or motivation. Further evidence and alternative explanations matter.

    2. How should the apparently conflicting newer results affect your view?

      Compare the pooled dyadic tasks with later group settings and with unequal rewards on only 25% or 50% of trials. These designs test related but distinct conditions.

      Evidence

      2024: pooled accept/reject data across 23 studies and 18 species did not show a statistically clear inequity effect.

      Scope: This pooled result concerns the included tasks and data. It is not a census of every possible animal response.

      • No evidence for inequity aversion in non-human animals: a meta-analysis of accept/reject paradigmsPrimary empirical research · Proceedings of the Royal Society B 291, 20241452; individual-participant data, 23 studies and 18 species
        What this source supports

        Reports no statistically clear pooled evidence of inequity aversion in studied accept/reject tasks.

        Access: Indexed primary full text and abstract inspected; PMC page returned an access challenge on this pass

        Limits: A null result is not proof of no effect or no capacity in all paradigms; samples and available data limit inference.

      Evidence

      A 2025 bonobo study manipulated human versus machine reward delivery; the response pattern challenged social disappointment alone but only partly matched inequity-aversion predictions.

      Scope: Six newly tested bonobos in one captive group. An older sample was pooled for one analysis; methods and limitations were inspected, without reanalysis.

      • Bonobos respond aversively to unequal reward distributionsPrimary empirical research · Proceedings of the Royal Society B 292, 20242873; §§2–4, subjects, controls, results and limitations
        What this source supports

        Two bonobo studies report unequal-reward responses; a human/machine manipulation challenges the social-disappointment explanation.

        Access: Primary full-text indexed methods/results/discussion inspected in pass003; no independent reanalysis

        Limits: Six newly tested bonobos in one group; one analysis pooled an older sample. Study 2 was only partly consistent with inequity aversion; partner variation and task constraints limit generalisation.

      Evidence

      2026: the chimpanzee group study (27 individuals) reported refusals mainly for low-value food, with responses related to social bonds.

      Scope: Selected full-text sections inspected. Social bonds were not randomly assigned; group participation differs from the pooled dyadic tasks.

      • Chimpanzees’ responses to inequity in a group context are influenced by food quality and their relationships with the group members presentPrimary empirical research · Proceedings of the Royal Society B 293, 20260397; selected §§2–4, design, measures and discussion
        What this source supports

        Reports context-sensitive responses in a group of chimpanzees.

        Access: Pass003: selected methods, results and discussion inspected in publisher-provided full text hosted on ResearchGate; no data reanalysis

        Limits: Voluntary participation in six captive groups; affiliation is not experimentally assigned. Advantageous refusals were too rare for statistical analysis. These measures do not establish a justice concept.

      Evidence

      September 2026: four primate species showed no increased refusals in the tested 25% and 50% unequal-trial conditions.

      Scope: A null refusal outcome does not show that the subjects never noticed a disparity.

      • No Response to Inconsistently Experienced Inequity in Four Primate SpeciesPrimary empirical research · American Journal of Primatology; DOI 10.1002/ajp.70196; abstract
        What this source supports

        At 25% or 50% unequal trials the studied primates did not increase refusals relative to controls.

        Access: Publisher and PubMed primary-study abstracts inspected; full text not reviewed

        Limits: Null refusal effects do not establish absence of noticing or other responses; task frequency and measures matter.

      Compare the tasks and avoid a universal conclusion.

      The pooled null and context-sensitive results both deserve attention. A task’s result limits claims about that task, not every possible social capacity.

    3. Suppose some fairness-related capacities have evolutionary roots. What follows about moral authority?

      Distinguish an explanation of how a response developed from a reason that a rule is justified.

      We still need reasons for whose claims count and how they are weighed.

      That supports the exhibit’s argument: shared capacities may help us form norms, while their authority needs a substantive account.

    Three values to consider

    Careful observation

    Separate behaviour from interpretation.

    A refusal is observable; its meaning needs controls and comparisons. Keep alternative explanations open.

    Context matters

    Results can depend on the task.

    Food value, partners and the frequency of unequal trials may change responses. Neither a positive nor a null result establishes every species’ capacities.

    From impulse to justification

    Why should a norm bind?

    Shared capacities can inform the story of morality, but a defended account of standing, reasons and authority is still needed.

    02 · Beyond “Just Preference”

    A life is more than a taste.

    Consider deliberately killing a non-threatening person for convenience. The example excludes self-defence and disputed end-of-life cases.

    Rejecting one account of moral truth does not erase the stakes or settle the reasons.

    What remains when approval changes? Commitment

    The loss of a person’s future, the violation of their agency, the effects on those who depend on them, and the threat posed to others do not disappear because an aggressor approves. To condemn the act, we add an explicit premise: those interests count, and another person’s comparable interests cannot be dismissed merely because they are not mine. This is a moral commitment with reasons behind it, not a fact manufactured by agreement.

    • Contractualism and UtilitarianismPrimary philosophy · In Utilitarianism and Beyond, pp. 103–128; indexed opening discussion of justification and moral theory
      What this source supports

      A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

      Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    • Moral RealismPrimary philosophy · The Philosophical Review 95(2), 163–207; especially pp. 164–176 and 189–198
      What this source supports

      A naturalist, relational account that permits criticism of actual approval.

      Access: Full primary article retrieved; selected sections on objectivity, informed interests and social rationality inspected in pass002

      Limits: Selected-section review, not an exhaustive assessment. The natural basis of interests must not be confused with approval creating the truth.

    Why the dessert analogy fails Argument

    Preferring one flavour and imposing an irreversible loss on another person differ in what is at stake, who bears the cost, and whether refusal is possible. Calling both “preferences” does not erase these differences. The intersubjective case asks for a justification to the person whose life is taken. The objection “I do not care” reveals the authority problem; it does not answer that person’s complaint.

      What this source supports

      Access: Author-hosted symposium manuscript retrieved; argument about deliberation, construction and skepticism inspected in pass002

      Limits: A substantive critic’s argument, not a neutral verdict on Enoch or proof of error theory.

    Take the skeptic literally Definition

    An error theorist can deny that any act has moral wrongness without endorsing it or denying nonmoral reasons to oppose it. Constructivists and expressivists offer different accounts again. The exhibit rejects the inference from “no stance-independent moral facts” to “no serious reason against killing”. It has not proved that every possible agent must accept its reasons.

    Take a position

    1. Which account best explains your objection to killing a non-threatening person for convenience?

      The victim’s future and agency are at stake. Choose an account of the objection, not a level of moral concern.

      The victim has a claim that the aggressor cannot justify overriding for convenience.

      This gives the person’s interests standing against the aggressor’s preference. The force of that standing is the normative premise to defend.

      This step examines an inference, not whether the rejection itself is correct.

    3. The aggressor says, “I understand your reasons and still do not care.” What does that show?

      Distinguish a reason’s authority from its ability to motivate this particular person.

      It exposes the hardest question about the framework’s authority.

      Yes: a case addressed to shared commitments may not compel a person who rejects them. Other people can still deliberate about protection and resistance.

    4. Conditional follow-up. Shown when “The aggressor says, “I understand your reasons and still do not care.” What does that show?” → “It exposes the hardest question about the framework’s authority.” or “I see no categorical authority here, only reasons relative to commitments.”. In the interactive version, a changed trigger requires reconsidering dependent responses.

      The aggressor can escape punishment and rejects reciprocity. What remains of the victim’s claim?

      Keep the original case fixed: intentional killing of a non-threatening person for convenience. Stipulate that deterrence and advantage cannot move this aggressor. We must distinguish a reason’s authority, the person’s recognition of it, and the reasons others have to intervene.

      The victim’s claim remains a constraint even though the aggressor rejects it.

      The defended account locates the claim in the affected life and reciprocal justification, not in the aggressor’s consent. You still owe a defence of that authority to the skeptic.

    03 · The Strongest Realist Case

    Could everyone be wrong?

    A good argument must survive an opponent who understands it.

    Realism can share the method while contesting the foundation.

    A realist who takes people seriously Argument

    Railton’s naturalist realism ties value to facts about people’s interests and circumstances, including what fuller information would reveal. Present approval can be mistaken. Its claim is that natural facts ground the assessment, rather than approval creating those facts. This is a serious rival to construction, not a supernatural caricature.

    • Moral RealismPrimary philosophy · The Philosophical Review 95(2), 163–207; especially pp. 164–176 and 189–198
      What this source supports

      A naturalist, relational account that permits criticism of actual approval.

      Access: Full primary article retrieved; selected sections on objectivity, informed interests and social rationality inspected in pass002

      Limits: Selected-section review, not an exhaustive assessment. The natural basis of interests must not be confused with approval creating the truth.

    A procedure can depend on prior reasons Argument

    Scanlon allows a constructivist account of interpersonal right and wrong while arguing that it relies on reasons it does not construct. That is a stronger challenge than rejecting all shared justification. Enoch asks why a feature constitutive of agency should bind someone who questions that agency; he separately argues that objective normativity may be indispensable to deliberation. Merely calling our procedure unavoidable answers neither objection. Our account must defend its substantive standing and reciprocity premises, then the further claim that they are sufficient.

    Same verdict, different grounds Provisional

    The strongest rival accepts the victim’s standing, every relevant interest, accountable representation and the same justified outcome. They say independent normative truths make those considerations authoritative. They need no extra practical complaint. Our constitutive account instead places the ground in the justified relation itself. Its fit to answerability, repair and human authorship supports the proposal, but does not uniquely defeat this rival. A realist may also accept local construction while remaining realist about its starting reasons.

    Take a position

    1. Which realist challenge deserves the strongest answer?

      Choose the challenge you want the proposal to face.

      Natural facts about informed interests may make moral judgments true.

      The proposal needs to explain why construction is preferable to that identification. Social origins and relational content do not defeat it.

      A realist agrees with every safeguard here. What could favour the constitutive account over the discovery account?

      The rival grants harm, agency, public evidence, dissent and correction. The disagreement is explanatory: do justified relations make a standard valid, or do they help us recognise a validity that has a different basis?

      Adequate answers to affected people complete the work that interpersonal validity needs to do.

      This defends sufficiency through the role of moral demands. The realist may accept the practical role while denying that it exhausts what makes the judgment true.

    3. Conditional follow-up. Shown when “Which realist challenge deserves the strongest answer?” → “Natural facts about informed interests may make moral judgments true.” or “Our deliberation seems to presuppose reasons we do not create.” or “An entire society may be wrong, even if no one challenges it.” or “The practical framework may leave the metaphysical question unanswered.”. In the interactive version, a changed trigger requires reconsidering dependent responses.

      Two accounts accept exactly the same justified complaints and practical decision. What do you think makes that decision morally valid?

      Hold the affected interests, standing, safeguards, accountability and result fixed. One account grounds interpersonal validity in the reciprocally justified relation. The realist says independent normative truths explain why that relation and its reasons have authority. Neither owes a different practical verdict simply to remain a rival.

      Argument

      The distinction is between the ground of validity and a way of learning what is valid. A constituted standard can still have answers that people must discover.

      Scope: The decision is hypothetical. Sources inform the distinction, not the invented facts.

      • Authority and Second-Personal Reasons for ActingPrimary philosophy: author manuscript · Reasons for Action, chapter 7, pp. 134–154; accessed manuscript pp. 1–7 and 13–16
        What this source supports

        Distinguishes reasons for beneficial compliance from a person’s standing to demand compliance and hold another accountable.

        Access: Yale-hosted full manuscript available; selected passages on authority, accountability and expertise inspected in pass004; Cambridge publication record verified

        Limits: The interdefinition of valid demand and authority does not establish everyone’s standing or prove antirealism. The 2006 book and 2013 reprint were not reviewed in full.

      • The Justification of Basic Rights: A Discourse-Theoretical ApproachPrimary philosophy: author manuscript · Netherlands Journal of Legal Philosophy 45(3), 7–28; manuscript §§1–2, pp. 1–9; especially p. 8
        What this source supports

        Access: Berkeley-hosted full manuscript available; selected grounding passages inspected in pass004; publisher-indexed PDF verified publication metadata but direct publisher retrieval failed

        Limits: Focuses on basic legal rights of recognised members; the membership question is bracketed. Not a premise-free derivation, proof of all moral constructivism or independent review of every rival rights theory.

    4. What work can an evolutionary explanation do in this debate?

      Street’s challenge concerns whether the origins of judgment fit a claim to reliable access. It is a philosophical argument with empirical premises.

      It can challenge reliability if the explanation disconnects judgment from truth.

      The argument needs that disconnection and an account of how much independent correction is available. Evolutionary influence alone is not the whole argument.

      reflection

      • A Darwinian Dilemma for Realist Theories of ValuePrimary philosophy · Philosophical Studies 127, 109–166; §§1–3, 5–7; pass003 adds §5, pp. 123–124 and §10, pp. 152–155
        What this source supports

        Challenges evolutionary reliability and sketches how actual evaluative error can survive an account grounded in a wider system of attitudes.

        Access: Full primary article retrieved; selected sections inspected in pass002; reflective-equilibrium and concluding antirealist passages inspected in pass003

        Limits: Evolutionary causes alone do not refute realism. The concluding sketch is not a full defence of one constructivism and does not itself establish MORARG’s equal-standing premise.

      Naturalist realism may explain why tracking some human interests helps us.

      This is a live realist response. It must show the relevant connection to moral truth rather than merely adaptive usefulness.

    5. What would most help distinguish construction from discovery?

      Suppose a careful inquiry yields the same judgment under both accounts. Agreement on that outcome does not decide which explanation is better.

      Compare how each account explains authority, error and correction.

      This shifts the contest to the work each theory does. Both explanations need scrutiny; the exhibit has not supplied an exhaustive comparison.

    Three values to consider

    Room for error

    Approval may be mistaken.

    Ask every account how people can be wrong even when sincere, coherent or united. An explanation of moral practice should preserve the possibility of criticism.

    Reliable access

    How would we know?

    Demand an account of evidence, reflection and correction from realism and construction alike. A psychological origin is relevant to reliability only through a further argument.

      What this source supports

      Access: Publisher and author abstracts inspected; preview preface checked in pass003; full book not read

      Limits: The 2009 lectures have a separate entry: the 2014 preface reports substantial revision, so their selected passages cannot be passed off as a full review of this book. Normative cognitivism is not interchangeable with Enoch’s robust realism.

    Discovery or construction

    One method, competing explanations.

    Shared inquiry may discover a prior truth, constitute a justified standard, or organise action without resolving either. Keep that difference visible when practical conclusions coincide.

      What this source supports

      A naturalist, relational account that permits criticism of actual approval.

      Access: Full primary article retrieved; selected sections on objectivity, informed interests and social rationality inspected in pass002

      Limits: Selected-section review, not an exhaustive assessment. The natural basis of interests must not be confused with approval creating the truth.

      What this source supports

    04 · Reasons in Both Directions

    What makes your claim count?

    The bridge from living together to owing one another reasons must be argued for.

    Reciprocity is a defended moral starting point, not a theorem extracted from cooperation.

    The demand for an exception Commitment

    Suppose you demand protection from another person’s interference but claim the freedom to impose comparable interference on them. What relevant difference justifies that exception? “It is my interest” explains your motivation, but gives the other person no comparable protection. The case for reciprocity is that a claim to govern both of you should withstand that exchange of positions. Equal standing is the premise being defended. Calling a rule morally authoritative over others, rather than merely advantageous to you, commits this account to that reciprocal test.

    • Contractualism and UtilitarianismPrimary philosophy · In Utilitarianism and Beyond, pp. 103–128; indexed opening discussion of justification and moral theory
      What this source supports

      A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

      Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    The argument from answerability Argument

    The claim is about what makes a demand valid, not simply how to discuss it. The final step is an argued proposal; the earlier points do not force every rival to accept it.

    1. An answer is owed to someone

      An expert can know what works without a right to command you. A person wronged can have a claim to restraint, an answer or repair. Interpersonal moral authority has this relational structure.

    2. Comparable claims deserve standing

      Power or inherited rank alone cannot answer another person’s comparable burden. We defend this commitment through agency and the demand for a relevant difference, not a deduction from self-interest.

    3. Justify the demand to those it governs

      Examine interests, alternatives, representation and unequal burdens under conditions people can challenge. Neither a vote nor a confident prediction substitutes for that answerability.

    4. Treat the relation as the ground

      We propose that meeting those demands constitutes interpersonal authority. This explains who is owed what, why a denial of standing matters, and how a better complaint can correct an accepted rule.

    5. Keep the decisive premise visible

      This integrated explanation supports our constitutive proposal. A realist can explain the same relations through prior reasons. Explanatory fit is not yet proof of comparative superiority or a refutation of realism.

    Representation must be answerable too Commitment

    Support a person’s own communication first. When that is impossible, distinguish known wishes, present welfare and prospective interests; disclose the proxy’s conflicts; allow independent challenge; and revisit the decision when the person can answer. These safeguards follow our commitments to agency and protection. They do not make a proxy’s preference into consent. Infants, future people and animals raise different questions of standing and weight: inability to bargain does not erase interests, but representation alone does not settle their claims.

      Limits: An ethical framework, not empirical proof of its own premises. Official or legal status does not establish moral truth.

      What this source supports

      Limits: These are 2009 lectures, not the revised 2014 book. Selected passages do not provide a complete theory of conflicting claims or settle the prior-reasons objection.

    Adequate by which conditions? Commitment

    State the tests before the verdict. These requirements constrain a justification, but judgments of standing and weight remain open to challenge. Passing a checklist is not proof of validity.

    1. Find the affected claims

      Include serious indirect effects and people unable to speak. Check evidence and uncertainty, support communication, and make proxies answerable.

    2. Compare the burden and alternatives

      Consider inaction, severity, likelihood, reversibility, agency and special responsibilities. Explain relevant differences; do not hide a moral weighting in a factual label.

    3. Make challenge effective

      Audit coercion, retaliation and control of information. Let the people bearing costs challenge the evidence and the standard, and identify what would change the decision.

    4. Allow a genuine failure

      If a well-supported wrong survives the stated tests, revise their sufficiency or scope. Do not automatically rename it a procedural defect. Inclusive discussion alone cannot justify a severe injury for many trivial conveniences.

    Take a position

    1. You demand protection for your interests. What could justify treating another person’s comparable claim differently?

      Comparable does not mean identical. Special obligations, capacity and relevant circumstances may make a difference.

      A relevant difference I can explain to the person bearing the cost.

      This is the proposed discipline of reciprocity. It allows justified distinctions while demanding more than who happens to hold power.

      This extends standing beyond active speakers. It still needs safeguards against a representative inventing or suppressing the person’s interests.

      What gives someone who cannot currently answer a moral claim on the decision-makers?

      Consider a child, a temporarily unconscious adult and a future person separately. None can answer this decision now. Prior wishes, current interests and prospective interests are different evidence; a proxy’s own preferences cannot simply stand in for them.

      Affected interests can ground a claim before any ability to answer is present.

      This extends standing beyond bargaining power. It requires accountable representation and a separate argument about the scope and weight of each kind of interest.

      An inclusive process permits a severe bodily burden for many trivial conveniences. What should this challenge force us to examine?

      Hypothetical: the facts and alternatives were examined, no affected claim was omitted, and everyone could challenge the proposed rule without retaliation. Its defenders consciously give many tiny benefits enough weight to override one person’s serious burden. This does not stipulate that their weighting is adequate; it tests what the formal safeguards leave unresolved.

      Argument

      The scenario is a test of procedural sufficiency, not evidence from the historical or scientific cases. Equal standing and the weight of harms remain independently challengeable moral premises.

      Scope: The decision is hypothetical. Sources inform the distinction, not the invented facts.

    5. Which part of reciprocity most strongly supports your commitment, if any?

      Choose a reason you can defend, rather than treating any answer as proof that the whole framework is true.

      Mutual protection makes shared constraints worth sustaining.

      A powerful practical reason. Its limit is the person who can exploit others safely and those with nothing to offer in return.

    Three values to consider

    Reciprocity

    Ask for a relevant difference.

    When a rule treats comparable interests differently, require an explanation beyond whose interests they are. Relevant needs and responsibilities can justify differences; bare power cannot do that work within this commitment.

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    Standing without power

    Interests count before bargaining begins.

    Include those who cannot negotiate or threaten retaliation. Grounding standing in affected interests requires further judgments about scope and weight, but avoids making vulnerability a reason for exclusion.

    Care and impartiality

    Special ties meet wider claims.

    Family, dependence and promises can create particular responsibilities. Ask how far those responsibilities justify burdens on outsiders, rather than assuming either perfect impartiality or unlimited loyalty.

    05 · A Framework with a Conscience

    Shared standards. Personal judgment.

    Neither inherited rules nor private certainty can do all the work.

    Responsibility requires judgment within and sometimes against the shared rule.

    Sometimes a decision helps make what is owed Argument

    Suppose two allocation schemes are equally defensible. A justified selection procedure can establish which one people should follow, within its limits. It helps determine an obligation, rather than uncover a unique scheme that was already mandatory. This is constitutive work within morality. It does not ground every basic constraint: legitimate decisions, promises and consent depend on conditions that need their own defence, and a realist can recognise those powers too. A counterfactual justification is also different from an actual agreement.

      What this source supports

    Compare claims, then own the decision Provisional

    Compare feasible alternatives, including delay. Ask who bears which loss, how severe, likely and reversible it is, what agency is restricted, and which special responsibilities apply. On this account, minor convenience normally cannot justify another person’s grave injury; that priority is a defended judgment, not arithmetic. Numbers can matter among comparable claims without making every small benefit outweigh a serious burden. Where reasonable rankings survive, justify a decision procedure and acknowledge what it leaves unresolved. No complete metric is supplied here.

    Care and anger can carry information Argument

    A person’s distress may expose a cost that an abstract rule concealed. Calmness does not guarantee sound reasoning, and anger does not erase relevant experience. Test factual claims and inferences while hearing the person. Knowledge, capacity, coercion and available options matter when assigning responsibility; saying “the rules told me to” does not by itself resolve it.

    Take a position

    1. A friend’s confidence suggests a credible risk of serious harm to another person. What would guide your decision?

      Hypothetical exercise: neither certainty of harm nor an easy harmless option is assumed. Consider time, reliability, privacy and the least intrusive protective response.

      Prioritise protection, disclosing only what a proportionate response needs.

      You give weight to the threatened person. Explain the evidential threshold and why a less intrusive response would not adequately protect them.

      Anger can direct attention to a neglected burden. Listening seriously remains compatible with checking causation or challenging a proposed remedy.

    3. An established rule conflicts with your considered judgment. Where does responsibility sit?

      Assume you have some ability to act, but refusal may have costs.

      I must assess the rule and explain my action within my actual options.

      Shared authorship does not remove personal judgment. Capacity, knowledge, coercion and available alternatives affect how much responsibility is appropriate.

    Three values to consider

    Personal judgment

    The rule cannot decide for you.

    Interpret the facts, scrutinise the rule and explain your choice. Institutions and relationships shape the options; they do not automatically remove responsibility. Knowledge, capacity and coercion remain relevant.

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    Trust and protection

    Some choices carry costs either way.

    Confidentiality protects agency and relationships; protection can require intervention. Look for proportionate responses, credible evidence and less intrusive options, while recognising that delay can also impose risk.

    Hear the experience

    Feeling can reveal a neglected cost.

    Take reports of distress seriously without treating emotion as proof of every explanation or as grounds for dismissal. Critique a specific claim; do not grade a person’s tone as moral worth.

    06 · Dissent, Power and Reform

    A community can fail its own people.

    A shared framework needs a route for criticizing the people who currently control it.

    Reform must be possible against consensus, not only after it changes.

    The dissenter’s complaint Argument

    An association excludes a minority from a common service, citing scarce capacity and past contributions. The minority documents serious burdens and disputes whether those considerations justify categorical exclusion. Examine the scarcity evidence, who was allowed to contribute, and less burdensome alternatives. Our equal-standing premise lets that complaint succeed without votes if the exclusion cannot be justified; it does not prejudge every allocation dispute. Actual acceptance and a justified standard remain different questions.

    • Wrongness and Reasons: A Re-examinationPrimary philosophy: chapter in publisher preview · Oxford Studies in Metaethics, volume 2, pp. 5–20; especially pp. 7–11 and 16–18
      What this source supports

      Reconsiders wrongness and reasons; treats contractualism as an account of an interpersonal form of wrongness rather than every possible moral ground.

      Access: Full chapter present in publisher/distributor preview; selected passages inspected in pass003

      Limits: MORARG owns its stronger constitutive proposal. This preview includes only the opening page of the following Enoch chapter and does not establish that chapter was read.

    • Moral RealismPrimary philosophy · The Philosophical Review 95(2), 163–207; especially pp. 164–176 and 189–198
      What this source supports

      A naturalist, relational account that permits criticism of actual approval.

      Access: Full primary article retrieved; selected sections on objectivity, informed interests and social rationality inspected in pass002

      Limits: Selected-section review, not an exhaustive assessment. The natural basis of interests must not be confused with approval creating the truth.

    Make disagreement capable of changing things Commitment

    An invitation to speak is weak if it brings retaliation, if evidence is controlled by the rule’s beneficiaries, or if the decision cannot be appealed. Independent scrutiny, protection for dissent and revisable decisions give objections a practical route. These are proposed safeguards, not a guarantee that the resulting society is just or that every disagreement ends.

      Limits: This is a political account, not a complete antirealist theory of morality.

    Why call a change better? Argument

    A new rule improves on an old one only relative to defended standards: fewer avoidable injuries, more agency, more defensible distributions of burdens, or other stated values. A realist can interpret that as discovering a prior truth. The intersubjective account interprets improvement through better justification. Both must show why the supposed improvement is more than the winners’ story.

    1. The association offers a reason for excluding a minority. What could decide whether the dissenter’s complaint succeeds?

      Hypothetical case: the association cites scarce capacity and past contributions. The minority describes substantial burdens, disputes who had the chance to contribute, and proposes less burdensome alternatives. The relevance and weight of the association’s reason remain contested.

      The complaint survives a fair comparison of burdens even without votes.

      This uses the proposed standing and justification premises. Its validity within the framework does not wait for political success.

    Three values to consider

    Protected dissent

    A complaint must be able to matter.

    Let a person challenge an accepted rule without requiring a majority on their side first. Protection from retaliation and a route to change the decision give that standing practical force.

    Who controls the test?

    A fair label can conceal control.

    Ask who defines respectable reasons, owns the evidence and decides an appeal. Inclusion is weak when the procedure cannot be challenged by those who bear its costs.

    Change and improvement

    Name the standard of progress.

    Explain why a new norm is better through its effects on agency, interests, burdens or another defended value. Winning or arriving later does not supply that explanation.

    07 · Tuskegee and Consent · CASE STUDY

    Whose body bears the cost?

    An actual research abuse tests the difference between institutional approval and justification.

    Neither institutional approval nor a signature substitutes for justification.

    Case study

    The USPHS study at Tuskegee

    Historical case, followed by an explicitly hypothetical research decision. The exercise does not ask you to defend the historical abuse.

    Case fact

    The study continued for four decades; participants’ informed consent was not obtained.

    • The Untreated Syphilis Study at Tuskegee TimelineOfficial historical record · Background; treatment spotlight; 1972 to present; page dated 4 September 2024
      What this source supports

      Documents missing informed consent and withheld treatment in the USPHS study.

      Access: Official full page inspected in pass002

      Limits: A current institutional historical account, not an independent examination of all original records. Modern decision scenarios in MORARG are explicitly hypothetical.

    Case fact

    Treatment was withheld after penicillin became available.

      What this source supports

      Documents missing informed consent and withheld treatment in the USPHS study.

      Access: Official full page inspected in pass002

      Limits: A current institutional historical account, not an independent examination of all original records. Modern decision scenarios in MORARG are explicitly hypothetical.

    Case fact

    Belmont links research ethics to autonomy and protection, risk and benefit, and the distribution of burdens.

    • The Belmont ReportPrimary ethical report · Part B §§1–3; Part C, informed consent, risk–benefit assessment and selection of subjects
      What this source supports

      Makes agency, protection, risk and distribution explicit in the ethics of research.

      Access: Official full text retrieved; principles and application sections inspected in pass002

      Limits: An ethical framework, not empirical proof of its own premises. Official or legal status does not establish moral truth.

    The decision

    For a new, independently reviewed study, how should a competent adult’s informed willingness be weighed against substantial risk and unequal access to alternatives?

    • Permit participation with stronger safeguards

      Preserves agency while requiring credible limits on pressure and harm.

    • Require a lower-risk design before recruitment

      Protects potential participants but may delay knowledge and access to an option they value.

    A shared ethical standard becomes useful when a person can invoke it against an institution.

    What this case does and does not show

    The historical record does not itself prove a metaethics. The new-study scenario is hypothetical and supplies no clinical recommendation.

    The documented case Evidence

    The U.S. Public Health Service study at Tuskegee ran from 1932 to 1972. Its initial participants were 600 Black men; informed consent was not obtained. After penicillin became an effective treatment, it was not offered to participants. These historical facts matter before we ask which moral theory best explains the wrong.

    • The Untreated Syphilis Study at Tuskegee TimelineOfficial historical record · Background; treatment spotlight; 1972 to present; page dated 4 September 2024
      What this source supports

      Documents missing informed consent and withheld treatment in the USPHS study.

      Access: Official full page inspected in pass002

      Limits: A current institutional historical account, not an independent examination of all original records. Modern decision scenarios in MORARG are explicitly hypothetical.

    The patient is not a research resource Commitment

    The argument against this conduct rests on agency, avoidable harm and the unfair placement of burdens. The Belmont Report later articulated respect for persons, beneficence and justice. That report supplies an explicit ethical framework; its official status does not make its principles self-proving. The people carrying the risk are owed reasons and a meaningful say.

    • The Belmont ReportPrimary ethical report · Part B §§1–3; Part C, informed consent, risk–benefit assessment and selection of subjects
      What this source supports

      Makes agency, protection, risk and distribution explicit in the ethics of research.

      Access: Official full text retrieved; principles and application sections inspected in pass002

      Limits: An ethical framework, not empirical proof of its own premises. Official or legal status does not establish moral truth.

    Consent is necessary work, not a magic signature Argument

    A modern hypothetical study offers a competent adult a risky experimental treatment. Refusing every risk may protect them but also defeat their informed choice; accepting a signature may ignore coercion, misunderstanding or an exploitative offer. Ask about comprehension, alternatives, fair selection and whether the risk is justified. This extension is a decision exercise, not a claim about a participant’s actual choice.

      Makes agency, protection, risk and distribution explicit in the ethics of research.

      Access: Official full text retrieved; principles and application sections inspected in pass002

      Limits: An ethical framework, not empirical proof of its own premises. Official or legal status does not establish moral truth.

    3. Conditional follow-up. Shown when “A competent adult understands a substantial research risk and still wants to participate. What should the review board do?” → “Allow participation only after credible safeguards and a justified risk assessment.” or “Give the adult’s informed decision greater weight than the board’s preference.” or “Seek clearer evidence about comprehension, alternatives and pressure.” or “Require a safer design before recruitment, despite delay.”. In the interactive version, a changed trigger requires reconsidering dependent responses.

      A redesigned study is acceptable enough to offer. Which data-use term would you now authorise?

      New hypothetical: following redesign and review, the protocol now meets the board’s minimum conditions for being offered. An informed adult may decline without losing ordinary care. Identifiable follow-up data improve the study’s reliability. Before recruitment the board must choose its withdrawal rule. No law or existing promise fixes that rule in this invented case.

      Argument

      The withdrawal terms are invented to test agency against the reliability of research. Belmont supplies broad principles; it does not decide this particular data-retention policy.

      Scope: The decision is hypothetical. Sources inform the distinction, not the invented facts.

      The participant has a final veto over every future use of their identifiable data, including analyses already begun.

      You make control over identifiable use a strict term of this study. Later scientific costs are a reason to seek permission, but not an exception to the term as stated.

      This protects the participant’s control. If you allowed a limited retention rule, you may nevertheless decide not to exercise that permission in this case.

    Three values to consider

    Informed agency

    A signature needs understanding and freedom.

    A person should be able to understand a consequential choice and refuse without improper pressure. Protection for diminished autonomy must support the person rather than silently replace their interests with institutional convenience.

      Access: Official full text retrieved; principles and application sections inspected in pass002

      Limits: An ethical framework, not empirical proof of its own premises. Official or legal status does not establish moral truth.

    Risk and paternalism

    Protect people without erasing their choices.

    An informed adult may have reasons to accept substantial risk. A review body must weigh that agency alongside evidence, alternatives and exploitation; neither prohibition nor permission is justified by a slogan.

    Fair research burdens

    Who pays for the knowledge?

    Scrutinise whose bodies and options make a study possible and who benefits. The value of knowledge does not by itself justify selecting those least able to resist the cost.
