    08 · Punishment and the Evidence · CASE STUDY

    What should punishment accomplish?

    A Norway study lets evidence challenge familiar slogans without deciding how much punishment anyone deserves.

    Evidence can change a policy premise while leaving a genuine conflict of values.

    Case study

    Incarceration, recidivism and employment in Norway

    A published empirical study followed by a hypothetical policy choice. It concerns system-level evidence, not sentencing advice for an individual.

    Case fact

    The main causal design estimates effects for defendants whose incarceration changes with the assigned judge’s stringency.

    • Incarceration, Recidivism, and EmploymentPrimary empirical study · Journal of Political Economy 128(4), 1269–1324; author manuscript dated 11 February 2019, §§2–3, 5–6 and 8
      What this source supports

      Estimates effects of incarceration in Norway using judge stringency; results vary by prior employment.

      Access: Author-hosted 92-page manuscript retrieved; selected design, local-effect interpretation, results and limitations inspected; 2020 journal abstract cross-checked

      Limits: Identifying assumptions and local population matter. No independent replication. Does not isolate one rehabilitation programme, estimate longer-sentence effects, or warrant direct transfer to every prison system.

    Case fact

    Its findings differ by prior employment, and the authors caution against universal generalisation.

    • Incarceration, Recidivism, and EmploymentPrimary empirical study · Journal of Political Economy 128(4), 1269–1324; author manuscript dated 11 February 2019, §§2–3, 5–6 and 8
      What this source supports

      Estimates effects of incarceration in Norway using judge stringency; results vary by prior employment.

      Access: Author-hosted 92-page manuscript retrieved; selected design, local-effect interpretation, results and limitations inspected; 2020 journal abstract cross-checked

      Limits: Identifying assumptions and local population matter. No independent replication. Does not isolate one rehabilitation programme, estimate longer-sentence effects, or warrant direct transfer to every prison system.

    The decision

    A justice committee must decide whether to prioritise rehabilitation, desert, or alternatives to custody while protecting the public and respecting liberty.

    • Prioritise prevention and reintegration

      Requires credible evidence and safeguards against imposing excessive burdens for aggregate benefits.

    • Make proportionate desert the first constraint

      Must still explain which hardships are deserved and how avoidable future harm matters.

    • Test alternatives to custody

      Preserves liberty where justified, while requiring evidence about protection and implementation.

    Public justification exposes both the empirical forecast and the value that makes it relevant.

    What this case does and does not show

    A quasi-experimental estimate depends on identifying assumptions and its population. This study does not isolate a particular programme or settle the ethics of punishment.

    • Incarceration, Recidivism, and EmploymentPrimary empirical study · Journal of Political Economy 128(4), 1269–1324; author manuscript dated 11 February 2019, §§2–3, 5–6 and 8

    More than a comparison of prisoners Evidence

    Bhuller and colleagues used differences in judges’ sentencing stringency, with a design based on assignment of cases to judges, to estimate imprisonment’s effects in Norway. Their 2020 paper reports lower subsequent offending for the defendants affected by that variation. The design matters: people who enter prison differ from those who do not, so a raw comparison can mislead.

    • Incarceration, Recidivism, and EmploymentPrimary empirical study · Journal of Political Economy 128(4), 1269–1324; author manuscript dated 11 February 2019, §§2–3, 5–6 and 8
      What this source supports

      Estimates effects of incarceration in Norway using judge stringency; results vary by prior employment.

      Access: Author-hosted 92-page manuscript retrieved; selected design, local-effect interpretation, results and limitations inspected; 2020 journal abstract cross-checked

      Limits: Identifying assumptions and local population matter. No independent replication. Does not isolate one rehabilitation programme, estimate longer-sentence effects, or warrant direct transfer to every prison system.

    One result is not a universal policy Evidence

    The estimated benefits were concentrated among people not employed before incarceration; previously employed defendants experienced employment losses. The study examines imprisonment in a particular system. It does not randomly assign a rehabilitation programme, establish that every prison helps, or tell us the effect of an additional year of punishment.

    • Incarceration, Recidivism, and EmploymentPrimary empirical study · Journal of Political Economy 128(4), 1269–1324; author manuscript dated 11 February 2019, §§2–3, 5–6 and 8
      What this source supports

      Access: Author-hosted 92-page manuscript retrieved; selected design, local-effect interpretation, results and limitations inspected; 2020 journal abstract cross-checked

      Limits: Identifying assumptions and local population matter. No independent replication. Does not isolate one rehabilitation programme, estimate longer-sentence effects, or warrant direct transfer to every prison system.

    Victims, liberty and a future outside prison Commitment

    Protecting future victims supports taking prevention seriously. Proportionate accountability may matter even when it has no extra deterrent effect. Liberty and the offender’s prospects constrain what may be imposed for others’ benefit. A defensible policy must address all of these claims and compare feasible alternatives; a favoured result cannot silently select the moral weights.

    Take a position

    1. What claim is the Norway study strong enough to support?

      Its judge-based design is more informative than a raw prisoner/non-prisoner comparison, but it retains assumptions and a bounded population.

      Evidence

      The authors estimate lower reoffending in the studied Norwegian setting, with effects differing by prior employment.

      Scope: Selected methods and results inspected; no independent replication. This is not an estimate for every prisoner or every country.

      Imprisonment in this setting can reduce later offending for affected defendants.

      This respects the estimated comparison and its scope. It is a reason to examine policy assumptions, not a universal endorsement of custody.

    2. How should the committee use this evidence when choosing a punishment policy?

      Hypothetical policy exercise: retain public protection and due process; compare the burdens on victims, defendants and others.

      Prioritise credible reductions in future harm and opportunities to reintegrate.

      This gives consequences weight. Explain constraints on how much liberty may be taken for aggregate benefits and what alternatives were considered.

      In a new sentencing hypothetical, how should a maximum justified by desert constrain prevention?

      Assume a fair assessment fixes a maximum deserved custodial sentence. We are testing the relation between that maximum and preventive benefit, not estimating a sentence or claiming the Norway study provides one. Feasible noncustodial protection remains relevant but may be costly.

      Once this deserved maximum is fixed, preventive benefit never justifies exceeding it in this case.

      You adopt a strict ceiling. A later threat can strengthen the case for other protection, but cannot alone authorise extra punishment under the principle you selected.

      Keep punishment within the fixed maximum and bear the remaining risk while pursuing other protection.

      You retain the ceiling despite a serious cost. A defensible position must acknowledge the people exposed to that risk rather than making them disappear.

    5. A victim wants a harsher penalty, while evidence suggests no extra protection from the additional hardship. What should follow?

      Hypothetical stipulation: assume separate, credible local evidence shows no added protection from this extra hardship. This is not a finding of the Norway study. Take the victim’s experience seriously while assessing the state’s coercion.

      Recognise the harm but require a further justification for the extra punishment.

      You separate a person’s warranted grievance from the permissible scope of state coercion. Explain which account of punishment supplies that limit.

        Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

      Extra punishment may be justified by desert even without preventive benefit.

      A contested normative position, not refuted by a result about reoffending. Its burden is to show why this additional hardship is proportionate and deserved.

      contested

        What this source supports

        A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

        Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

        Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

      Prioritise meaningful repair and recognition alongside an independently justified sentence.

      This addresses the victim without treating severity as the only form of recognition. Feasibility, consent and the limits of repair still matter.

      I need to hear the grievance and the reasons for the existing sentence.

      Those details can matter. Anger alone neither establishes nor defeats the argument for a penalty.

        What this source supports

        Access: Abstract and metadata inspected in pass001; full article not newly reviewed

        Limits: Used as a limited background source, not a finding that emotion discredits reason or reasoning never changes judgment.

    Three values to consider

    Prevent future harm

    Outcomes matter to people who will suffer them.

    Take credible evidence about prevention seriously, including evidence that challenges a familiar punishment policy. Forecasts need limits, and prevention does not by itself justify every restriction of liberty.

    • Incarceration, Recidivism, and EmploymentPrimary empirical study · Journal of Political Economy 128(4), 1269–1324; author manuscript dated 11 February 2019, §§2–3, 5–6 and 8
      What this source supports

      Estimates effects of incarceration in Norway using judge stringency; results vary by prior employment.

      Access: Author-hosted 92-page manuscript retrieved; selected design, local-effect interpretation, results and limitations inspected; 2020 journal abstract cross-checked

      Limits: Identifying assumptions and local population matter. No independent replication. Does not isolate one rehabilitation programme, estimate longer-sentence effects, or warrant direct transfer to every prison system.

    Proportionate accountability

    Recognition is not unlimited severity.

    Ask what a person is responsible for and what response is deserved. A grievance deserves a hearing without automatically determining the degree of coercion others may impose.

    • Contractualism and UtilitarianismPrimary philosophy · In Utilitarianism and Beyond, pp. 103–128; indexed opening discussion of justification and moral theory
      What this source supports

      A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

      Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    Liberty and reintegration

    An offender still has a future.

    Compare custody with feasible alternatives and account for employment, relationships and a path back into shared life. Rehabilitation and liberty can conflict; identify the actual burdens and evidence.

    09 · The Ozone Commons · CASE STUDY

    Obligations across distance and time.

    A shared atmosphere gives people reasons to care about actions they cannot individually control.

    Shared action can be justified before everyone agrees about the ultimate source of value.

    Case study

    The Montreal Protocol and the ozone layer

    A real international response, with a hypothetical decision about distributing the cost of the next protective measure.

    Case fact

    The 2022 assessment finds progress in ozone recovery alongside significant remaining uncertainties.

    • Scientific Assessment of Ozone Depletion: 2022 — Executive SummaryPrimary official scientific assessment · Highlights 1–2; Executive Summary §3, stratospheric ozone; conditional future evolution
      What this source supports

      Reports progress in ozone recovery and falling controlled substances, with incomplete and regionally varied recovery.

      Access: Official executive-summary text inspected in pass002, including regional qualifications and conditional projections

      Limits: A dated scientific assessment, not live monitoring or a moral finding. Projections depend on assumptions and compliance; near-global total-column recovery had limited evidence and low confidence.

    Case fact

    Article 10 establishes a mechanism for financial and technical cooperation, including a Multilateral Fund.

    • Montreal Protocol, Article 10: Financial mechanismPrimary treaty text · Current consolidated Article 10, especially paragraphs 1–3, 6 and 9
      What this source supports

      An actual mechanism for financial and technical cooperation, including a Multilateral Fund.

      Access: Official consolidated article inspected in pass002; 1990 denotes introduction of the mechanism, not the date of every later amendment

      Limits: The text establishes institutional arrangements. It does not prove their fairness, implementation success, or moral authority.

    The decision

    A protective transition is feasible but costly. Choose a burden-sharing principle and account for people who cannot negotiate now.

    • Require the same contribution from everyone

      A uniform rule is clear, but may impose very unequal sacrifices.

    • Require more from those with capacity or responsibility

      Can address unequal burdens, but needs an argued basis and credible measurement.

    • Negotiate a feasible mixed rule with review

      May secure action sooner, while leaving an obligation to explain and repair unfair compromises.

    Socially created institutions can respond to human-independent physical facts without thereby settling stance-independent moral truth.

    What this case does and does not show

    The selected assessment is dated 2022; this room is not a live atmospheric monitor. Its projections are conditional, and the treaty’s legal authority is not a proof of moral authority.

      Limits: A dated scientific assessment, not live monitoring or a moral finding. Projections depend on assumptions and compliance; near-global total-column recovery had limited evidence and low confidence.

    A response with observable consequences Evidence

    The WMO’s 2022 assessment reports declining controlled ozone-depleting substances and signs of ozone recovery, with important regional differences and uncertainty. Recovery is incomplete. This is evidence about a coordinated response and atmospheric processes, not a scientific measurement of moral rightness.

    • Scientific Assessment of Ozone Depletion: 2022 — Executive SummaryPrimary official scientific assessment · Highlights 1–2; Executive Summary §3, stratospheric ozone; conditional future evolution
      What this source supports

      Reports progress in ozone recovery and falling controlled substances, with incomplete and regionally varied recovery.

      Access: Official executive-summary text inspected in pass002, including regional qualifications and conditional projections

      Limits: A dated scientific assessment, not live monitoring or a moral finding. Projections depend on assumptions and compliance; near-global total-column recovery had limited evidence and low confidence.

    Cooperation needs a distribution of costs Argument

    The Montreal Protocol’s Article 10 provides financial and technical support for eligible developing-country parties. That institutional design makes a moral question visible: who should pay to change production, and why? Capacity, contribution to the problem and basic needs are different possible reasons. The existence of the treaty does not settle which allocation is fair.

    • Montreal Protocol, Article 10: Financial mechanismPrimary treaty text · Current consolidated Article 10, especially paragraphs 1–3, 6 and 9
      What this source supports

      An actual mechanism for financial and technical cooperation, including a Multilateral Fund.

      Access: Official consolidated article inspected in pass002; 1990 denotes introduction of the mechanism, not the date of every later amendment

      Limits: The text establishes institutional arrangements. It does not prove their fairness, implementation success, or moral authority.

    People outside the negotiating room Commitment

    Future people and people with little negotiating power still bear consequences. Our framework asks current decision-makers to represent those interests and explain trade-offs openly. Waiting for unanimous moral agreement can also impose a cost. Acting provisionally requires evidence, fair burden sharing and review; urgency is not a blank cheque for whoever claims to speak for the future.

    Take a position

    1. What does the ozone evidence establish for this argument?

      A coordinated institution can change physical outcomes. That does not make every institutional choice right.

      Evidence

      The 2022 assessment reports recovery progress, regional differences and unresolved uncertainties.

      Scope: Dated assessment, not current monitoring; projected recovery depends on future conditions.

      It is compatible with humans discovering a moral duty to prevent avoidable harm.

        Limits: Selected-section review, not an exhaustive assessment. The natural basis of interests must not be confused with approval creating the truth.

      The 2022 assessment shows that the ozone layer is already fully recovered everywhere.

    2. How would you distribute the cost of a further protective transition?

      Hypothetical extension: the measure is feasible but different parties have unequal resources and contributions to the problem.

      Require more from those better able to pay, while protecting basic needs.

      This can reduce unequal sacrifice. Explain why capacity creates this duty and how to avoid discouraging needed development.

      This connects responsibility to the burden. You still need fair measures of contribution, historical knowledge and present ability to pay.

    3. Conditional follow-up. Shown when “How would you distribute the cost of a further protective transition?” → “Require more from those better able to pay, while protecting basic needs.” or “Require more from those who contributed more to the problem.” or “Begin with an equal rule, allowing justified exceptions for severe hardship.” or “Negotiate a feasible mixture with transparent reasons and scheduled review.”. In the interactive version, a changed trigger requires reconsidering dependent responses.

      A proposed transition could protect the atmosphere while leaving some already poor households below a defined basic-needs floor. What principle governs this plan?

      A new hypothetical funding choice. The minimum covers food, shelter and essential care; the affected households already have little margin. This is not a finding about the Montreal Protocol’s actual allocations. The policy’s environmental gains and present burdens both matter.

      This plan must never knowingly push those households below that floor, even to secure its environmental gains.

      You choose a strict protection for this plan. It limits the acceptable financing or timing, and may require accepting a cost elsewhere.

Delay would cause significant additional environmental damage; there is no immediately feasible plan that avoids both costs. These are stipulated facts to expose the conflict, not a forecast from the 2022 assessment.

      Delay this plan while seeking financing, accepting the additional environmental damage.

      You protect the households from the stated burden while accepting a cost to others. Their environmental claims still deserve an answer.

    5. How should people who cannot negotiate now affect the decision?

      Future harms may be uncertain, but choosing delay also distributes risk. Do not assume the future has no claims because it has no representative at the table.

      Give serious irreversible risks weight before certainty is available.

      A precautionary priority. Explain proportionality, the evidence threshold and how immediate basic needs will remain protected.

      This makes absence less likely to mean exclusion. Representatives remain accountable for their assumptions and may disagree.

    6. Conditional follow-up. Shown when “How should people who cannot negotiate now affect the decision?” → “Use explicit representation, scenarios and review to expose those burdens.” or “Give serious irreversible risks weight before certainty is available.” or “I doubt our present framework can fully determine obligations to future people.”. In the interactive version, a changed trigger requires reconsidering dependent responses.

      Do these future people already have reciprocal claims on the present decision-makers?

      They do not yet exist and have no present ability to answer. The question concerns a reciprocal claim now, not whether future welfare matters, whether these people will later become agents, or whether a present person prudently values the future. Keep those grounds distinct.

      They have reciprocal claims now despite having no present ability to answer.

      You allow reciprocal standing without present participation. Defend how their interests are specified and represented, including uncertainty over who will exist.

      This can fit a narrower account of reciprocity with other moral grounds for protection. It need not mean ignoring the future.

    Three values to consider

    A shared world

    Your action can affect distant lives.

    Take the physical links among people’s choices seriously. Shared vulnerability creates a practical problem that private preference alone does not resolve; a moral response also needs a defended account of standing.

      What this source supports

      Reports progress in ozone recovery and falling controlled substances, with incomplete and regionally varied recovery.

      Access: Official executive-summary text inspected in pass002, including regional qualifications and conditional projections

      Limits: A dated scientific assessment, not live monitoring or a moral finding. Projections depend on assumptions and compliance; near-global total-column recovery had limited evidence and low confidence.

    Fair transition

    Equal rules can impose unequal sacrifices.

    Compare capacity, responsibility and basic needs when allocating costs. A feasible compromise needs reasons and review; the existence of an agreement does not certify its fairness.

      What this source supports

      Access: Official consolidated article inspected in pass002; 1990 denotes introduction of the mechanism, not the date of every later amendment

      Limits: The text establishes institutional arrangements. It does not prove their fairness, implementation success, or moral authority.

    The absent future

    Who represents people not here yet?

    Make future burdens explicit through evidence, scenarios and accountable representation. Uncertainty is a reason to judge carefully, not automatically to assign the future zero weight.

    10 · Objections That Remain

    The hardest questions stay in the room.

    Ten serious objections, the strongest reply available here, and a burden the reply leaves. These are authored arguments, not quotations.

    A position earns confidence by exposing its weakest premise.

    Why should the powerful care?

    Objection: A person can understand reciprocity and still profit from domination. Why is your framework binding on them?

    The reply: The victim’s life and agency supply a claim that the aggressor’s indifference does not answer. We defend reciprocal justification as what gives an interpersonal demand authority; a threat alone supplies power. Others can have grounds to resist even if the aggressor remains unmoved.

    What still needs defending: Enoch’s challenge remains: neither constitutive agency nor inescapable participation automatically supplies a reason to comply. This account defends standing substantively; it has not derived it from premises every possible agent accepts.

    • Agency, Shmagency: Why Normativity Won’t Come from What Is Constitutive of ActionPrimary philosophy: journal text through public mirror · The Philosophical Review 115(2), 169–198; §3, pp. 177–180; discussion of inescapability; §§7–8, pp. 192–196
      What this source supports

      Constitutive features of agency do not by themselves establish their authority; the later epistemic appeal to deliberative indispensability is a separate argument.

      Access: Selected primary text inspected through an indexed public mirror; title and publication checked against author records; publisher PDF not retrieved

      Limits: Mirror OCR can contain errors. This is a selected-argument review, not a full reading of Taking Morality Seriously or all replies to constitutivism.

      What this source supports

    • Error TheoryPrimary philosophy by an error theorist · International Encyclopedia of Ethics entry; author manuscript, pp. 1–3 and pp. 6–7 on conceptual and ontological premises
      What this source supports

      Moral error theory denies moral properties, rather than granting that everything is morally permissible.

      Access: Author-hosted manuscript retrieved; selected conceptual and practical distinctions inspected in pass002

      Limits: Error theory, antirealism and indifference are distinct. The author site labels papers generally as drafts; consult the publication for the definitive text.

    Was the abuse wrong before anyone objected?

    Objection: If acceptance makes rightness, unanimous approval would make a cruel practice right. A reformer needs more than a future majority.

    The reply: The proposal makes justified standards answer to affected interests and agency; actual approval is not the test. A complaint can be warranted before it is heard or accepted.

    What still needs defending: Those constraints must remain defensible even when rejected. This explains the project’s judgment; it does not compel a realist to accept its metaphysical explanation.

    • Moral RealismPrimary philosophy · The Philosophical Review 95(2), 163–207; especially pp. 164–176 and 189–198
      What this source supports

      A naturalist, relational account that permits criticism of actual approval.

      Access: Full primary article retrieved; selected sections on objectivity, informed interests and social rationality inspected in pass002

      Limits: Selected-section review, not an exhaustive assessment. The natural basis of interests must not be confused with approval creating the truth.

    • Contractualism and UtilitarianismPrimary philosophy · In Utilitarianism and Beyond, pp. 103–128; indexed opening discussion of justification and moral theory
      What this source supports

      A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

      Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    Have you built your answer into the procedure?

    Objection: If a fair procedure is defined by equality and respect, its fair conclusion may merely restate your starting morality.

    The reply: Test the premises against rival principles, contrary cases, excluded people’s experience and accurate evidence. This is wider than repeating a favoured conclusion. Standing remains a substantive commitment: hereditary status cannot alone justify dismissing the complaint against that status.

    What still needs defending: Mutual support can justify a belief without constituting its truth. Scanlon insists on that distinction; a coherent cruel system also shows why coherence is insufficient. Defend which challenges count and the further constitutive step.

      Limits: The accessed text is a draft, not a line-by-line comparison with the final publication. A local interpersonal account avoids the global target only by openly defending its remaining normative premises.

    Why not call those human facts moral facts?

    Objection: Facts about informed interests and social relations may ground truth, independent of what people approve. A realist can accept the same claims, accountability and practical results.

    The reply: The constitutive account explains authority through justified relations: what is owed, to whom, and why repair or correction is called for. This is positive explanatory fit, but the strongest relational realist can preserve it.

    What still needs defending: The case for our grounding remains a defended interpretation, not a demonstrated failure of every realist explanation. A realist can accept local constitution while grounding its conditions independently.

      What this source supports

      Access: Yale-hosted full manuscript available; selected passages on authority, accountability and expertise inspected in pass004; Cambridge publication record verified

      Limits: The interdefinition of valid demand and authority does not establish everyone’s standing or prove antirealism. The 2006 book and 2013 reprint were not reviewed in full.

    Does deliberation already need objective reasons?

    Objection: In deciding what to do, I try to find a better answer. Perhaps irreducible normative truths make that activity possible.

    The reply: A conditional account can ask what a defensible procedure would yield, even before anyone runs it. That allows discovery of an answer within construction. It does not yet show that the reasons selecting the procedure are themselves constructed.

    What still needs defending: Scanlon accepts construction of interpersonal right and wrong while retaining prior reasons. Enoch challenges global construction and argues separately from deliberative indispensability. MORARG must defend its sufficiency claim against these rivals, not answer them by calling all reasons inventions.

    What if careful people still disagree?

    Objection: Accurate facts and fair discussion may leave conflicting views about rights, welfare and desert. Action cannot always wait.

    The reply: Narrow the disagreement, identify costs of delay, consider reversible options, and justify a provisional decision procedure. A decision can be legitimate without making every losing moral argument false.

    What still needs defending: The conditions of legitimate coercion remain substantive. Consensus, majority voting and reasonable rejection each need limits and a defence.

      Limits: This is a political account, not a complete antirealist theory of morality.

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    Who decides which reasons are respectable?

    Objection: The powerful may control the language, evidence and standards of reasonableness, while appearing open to consultation.

    The reply: Protect refusal and dissent; examine who bears the costs and controls the evidence; allow independent challenges and changes to the procedure itself.

    What still needs defending: These safeguards can fail or be captured. The exhibit offers design commitments, not a guarantee of justice or neutrality across cultures.

    Who speaks for those who cannot answer?

    Objection: Future people, children, some disabled people and animals cannot all participate in reciprocal discussion. Representation can become ventriloquism.

    The reply: Locate standing in affected interests rather than bargaining strength. Use evidence, multiple representatives, transparent assumptions and revision when the represented person can speak.

    What still needs defending: The scope and weight of interests need defence. Human interpersonal justification is not yet a complete theory of animal ethics or future-person identity.

    Does a demand for reasons silence suffering?

    Objection: A harmed person may be angry, unable to speak calmly, or unable to translate experience into the dominant vocabulary.

    The reply: A report of distress can itself be relevant evidence. Listen and investigate; assess a particular factual assertion or inference without grading the speaker’s tone or moral worth.

    What still needs defending: Experience deserves uptake but does not automatically establish every causal claim or policy conclusion drawn from it. Fair hearing and critical assessment must work together.

    Are you preserving a useful mistake?

    Objection: If ordinary moral claims assert a kind of authority that does not exist, improving the practice does not make them true.

    The reply: We offer a constitutive account on which adequate interpersonal justification makes a human moral claim valid. If ordinary language demands more, that may require revising its interpretation. Practical usefulness alone is not its truth-maker.

    What still needs defending: Explain whether this retains ordinary morality or replaces it. Error theory about moral truth can coexist with serious concern and nonmoral reasons; it is not a diagnosis of indifference.

    Take a position

      Why its reasons bind a person who rejects its commitments.

      This is the central authority gap. The exhibit’s practical and interpersonal case does not close it by definition.

    2. Conditional follow-up. Shown when “Which remaining objection most threatens the proposed framework?” → “Why the procedure’s starting commitments deserve their privileged place.” or “Why its reasons bind a person who rejects its commitments.”. In the interactive version, a changed trigger requires reconsidering dependent responses.

      If every justification uses some standards, how should we distinguish a defensible starting point from a protected prejudice?

      The account proposes wide scrutiny: rival principles, experiences of those burdened, accurate evidence and cases that challenge our favoured conclusion. That can improve a view without deriving all reasons from a neutral starting point. The question is what sort of warrant this earns.

      Mutual support earns warrant only when the starting judgments remain vulnerable to counterexamples and excluded claims.

      This is a substantive defence of disciplined reflection. Coherence alone is insufficient; explain what would make you revise a cherished starting judgment.

    3. If your strongest objection remains, what should happen to your conclusion?

      An unanswered objection may defeat a claim, narrow it, or lower confidence without erasing everything learned.

      Keep the practical framework while withholding the stronger metaphysics.

      This is a coherent narrower conclusion. Be clear which authority claim you have retained and which remains open.

      They agree on a practice and may cooperate while disagreeing about what makes the practice right.

      This is a meaningful gain. Shared action can be assessed without claiming the participants now have the same metaethics.

    Three values to consider

    The authority question

    Why are these reasons binding?

    Distinguish a workable practice from a complete account of moral authority. A strong response must address a dissenter rather than define disagreement out of the procedure.

    • Error TheoryPrimary philosophy by an error theorist · International Encyclopedia of Ethics entry; author manuscript, pp. 1–3 and pp. 6–7 on conceptual and ontological premises
      What this source supports

      Moral error theory denies moral properties, rather than granting that everything is morally permissible.

      Access: Author-hosted manuscript retrieved; selected conceptual and practical distinctions inspected in pass002

      Limits: Error theory, antirealism and indifference are distinct. The author site labels papers generally as drafts; consult the publication for the definitive text.

      What this source supports

      Limits: The 2009 lectures have a separate entry: the 2014 preface reports substantial revision, so their selected passages cannot be passed off as a full review of this book. Normative cognitivism is not interchangeable with Enoch’s robust realism.

    Equal scrutiny

    Test the favoured view too.

    Apply standards of explanation and evidence to your own position as seriously as to its rivals. A useful example cannot become a metaphysical proof merely because its lesson is attractive.

    Disagreement and action

    A decision need not end the debate.

    Some choices cannot wait for complete agreement. Explain the legitimacy, burdens and reversibility of a provisional decision without pretending that the losing position has been refuted.

    11 · Your Provisional Position

    Choose what you can defend.

    A conclusion can be firm enough to guide action and limited enough to remain honest.

    Commit to reasons you can expose to challenge; do not mistake completion for proof.

    The case we have made Argument

    Our conclusion is constitutive: defensible relations among affected people make interpersonal moral standards valid. This account connects what is owed, who can demand it, who is owed repair, and how a complaint can overturn an accepted rule. Where several arrangements remain permissible, legitimate human agency can also help determine what is owed. We favour this integrated account of authority and responsibility beyond private preference. Its grounding premise is defended, not supplied by the empirical cases.

    • Authority and Second-Personal Reasons for ActingPrimary philosophy: author manuscript · Reasons for Action, chapter 7, pp. 134–154; accessed manuscript pp. 1–7 and 13–16
      What this source supports

      Distinguishes reasons for beneficial compliance from a person’s standing to demand compliance and hold another accountable.

      Access: Yale-hosted full manuscript available; selected passages on authority, accountability and expertise inspected in pass004; Cambridge publication record verified

      Limits: The interdefinition of valid demand and authority does not establish everyone’s standing or prove antirealism. The 2006 book and 2013 reprint were not reviewed in full.

    • The Justification of Basic Rights: A Discourse-Theoretical ApproachPrimary philosophy: author manuscript · Netherlands Journal of Legal Philosophy 45(3), 7–28; manuscript §§1–2, pp. 1–9; especially p. 8
      What this source supports

      Connects equal normative authority with reciprocal justification and explicitly states that constructed norms depend on prior normativity of principles and standing.

      Access: Berkeley-hosted full manuscript available; selected grounding passages inspected in pass004; publisher-indexed PDF verified publication metadata but direct publisher retrieval failed

      Limits: Focuses on basic legal rights of recognised members; the membership question is bracketed. Not a premise-free derivation, proof of all moral constructivism or independent review of every rival rights theory.

    • Being Realistic about Reasons: John Locke Lectures 4 and 5Primary philosophy: lecture manuscripts · Lecture 4, pp. 1–3, 13–20 and 24–26; Lecture 5, pp. 15–21
      What this source supports

      Distinguishes construction of interpersonal right and wrong from prior reasons, and epistemic justification from normative grounding; Lecture 5 examines contextual comparisons of reasons.

      Access: Official Oxford-hosted full lecture PDFs available; selected sections inspected in pass003

      Limits: These are 2009 lectures, not the revised 2014 book. Selected passages do not provide a complete theory of conflicting claims or settle the prior-reasons objection.

    What would change the case? Provisional

    A serious wrong that passes our stated conditions challenges their sufficiency. Two defensible but incompatible schemes may be alternatives, rather than two mandatory duties. A realist who accepts every practical reason can still give a rival grounding. Equal standing and the weight of claims remain substantive commitments; the argument defends them through reciprocal relations without proving that they lack any independent foundation. Local construction may coexist with realism about prior reasons. The broader antirealist ambition remains open.

    Make the conclusion your own Provisional

    Review the reasons you selected and the values you chose to carry. Keep a rival position, endorse this one, or suspend judgment. Ask which factual premise you would check next, which burden you are willing to impose, and who should be able to challenge you. The record preserves choices and explanations; it awards no philosophical victory or moral rank.

    Take a position

    1. Which position can you currently defend?

      You may have learned from a framework without accepting its strongest interpretation.

      Justifiability to those affected constitutes moral validity; personal judgment applies it.

      You endorse the stronger constitutive thesis. Its standing, reciprocity and sufficiency premises remain yours to defend; successful cases alone cannot prove them.

      A substantive argument for intersubjective moral validity, with its decisive premises exposed.

      The argument actually proposes what constitutes validity, while acknowledging that its premises and sufficiency claim remain contested. It does not amount to a conclusive refutation of realism.

        Limits: A substantive critic’s argument, not a neutral verdict on Enoch or proof of error theory.

      The evidence conclusively proves that objective moral truth is impossible.

      The evidence does not rule out realist explanations. “Objective” also needs its intended sense specified; the universal conclusion is not established.

      unsupported · claim requires repair

      I need another case or argument before deciding.

      Completion is not assent. Carry the question you want answered into your next inquiry.

      reflection

    Three values to consider

    A commitment you own

    Say what you can defend.

    Name your premises and the reasons supporting them. Keep the distinction between a position that guides your action and a conclusion that every opponent has been forced to accept.

    • Contractualism and UtilitarianismPrimary philosophy · In Utilitarianism and Beyond, pp. 103–128; indexed opening discussion of justification and moral theory
      What this source supports

      A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

      Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    A route to revision

    What could change your mind?

    Identify a fact, objection or neglected interest that would prompt reconsideration. Revision is substantive when a possible outcome could change the conclusion, not only its presentation.

    • Being Realistic about ReasonsPrimary work: author and publisher synopsis · Book abstract and contents; Harvard author abstract; preview preface describing revision of the lectures
      What this source supports

      Defends irreducible normative truths and a role for reflective equilibrium.

      Access: Publisher and author abstracts inspected; preview preface checked in pass003; full book not read

      Limits: The 2009 lectures have a separate entry: the 2014 preface reports substantial revision, so their selected passages cannot be passed off as a full review of this book. Normative cognitivism is not interchangeable with Enoch’s robust realism.

    An open foundation

    How much has the argument earned?

    You may accept shared justification while retaining realism or uncertainty. Keep the strongest remaining objection attached to the conclusion rather than treating completion as philosophical agreement.

    How the selected-claim checks work

    Only the explicitly stated combinations below trigger a comparison. A change of mind, a contextual distinction or a principled disagreement can be recorded. These explanations do not automatically prove a logical resolution. Checks use current answers only; responses to inactive or changed branches are retained separately.

    Possible inconsistency

    Reasons against the same killing

    The first answer retains your nonmoral reasons to oppose this act. The second says nobody has any reason to oppose the same act. Taken as current claims with “reason” used in the same sense, they cannot both hold.

    Scope: Only the specified convenience killing; no conclusion about error theory, antirealism or the person’s character follows. A changed view or distinction may explain the pair. Revising the outdated answer clears the active pair; an acknowledgement alone records an explanation and leaves it visible. A distinction must address the second answer’s unrestricted “any reason”.

    Exact selected-answer combination

    (“Which account best explains your objection to killing a non-threatening person for convenience?” → “I oppose the act for nonmoral reasons while doubting moral truth.”) AND (“What follows if you reject stance-independent moral facts?” → “That rejection by itself proves nobody has any reason to oppose the killing.”)

    • Error TheoryPrimary philosophy by an error theorist · International Encyclopedia of Ethics entry; author manuscript, pp. 1–3 and pp. 6–7 on conceptual and ontological premises
      What this source supports

      Moral error theory denies moral properties, rather than granting that everything is morally permissible.

      Access: Author-hosted manuscript retrieved; selected conceptual and practical distinctions inspected in pass002

      Limits: Error theory, antirealism and indifference are distinct. The author site labels papers generally as drafts; consult the publication for the definitive text.

    Possible inconsistency

    A final veto and continued identifiable use

    The chosen term gives this participant a final veto over every future identifiable use, including an analysis already begun. The proposed action continues exactly that use after permission is refused. The term or action must change, or a relevant distinction must be supplied.

    Scope: The same hypothetical study, same identifiable data and same future analysis; no new emergency, law or impossibility has been introduced. Revising the supported term is allowed and is different from claiming both govern unchanged.

    Exact selected-answer combination

    (“A redesigned study is acceptable enough to offer. Which data-use term would you now authorise?” → “The participant has a final veto over every future use of their identifiable data, including analyses already begun.”) AND (“The participant now requests deletion. The identifiable analysis is still under way. What should happen?” → “Continue this future identifiable use without the participant’s permission to preserve the study’s reliability.”)

    • The Belmont ReportPrimary ethical report · Part B §§1–3; Part C, informed consent, risk–benefit assessment and selection of subjects
      What this source supports

      Makes agency, protection, risk and distribution explicit in the ethics of research.

      Access: Official full text retrieved; principles and application sections inspected in pass002

      Limits: An ethical framework, not empirical proof of its own premises. Official or legal status does not establish moral truth.

    Possible inconsistency

    A retained ceiling and extra punishment

    Your earlier answer keeps punishment within the fixed ceiling, either without a prevention exception or with any separate protection requiring its own justification. The later action adds an explicitly punitive year beyond that same ceiling. Calling it separate protection cannot reconcile this particular stipulated action with the retained ceiling.

    Scope: The stipulated maximum is unchanged and the extra year is explicitly punishment. This does not compare general desert with rehabilitation, or use the Norway study to predict an extra year’s effects. A deliberate revision remains available.

    Exact selected-answer combination

    (“In a new sentencing hypothetical, how should a maximum justified by desert constrain prevention?” → “Once this deserved maximum is fixed, preventive benefit never justifies exceeding it in this case.” or “Keep punishment within the ceiling and justify any separate protective restriction on its own terms.”) AND (“Credible local evidence now predicts that an extra custodial year would prevent grave harm. Which constraint governs?” → “Authorise the extra punitive year beyond the still-fixed maximum to prevent that grave harm.”)

    • Contractualism and UtilitarianismPrimary philosophy · In Utilitarianism and Beyond, pp. 103–128; indexed opening discussion of justification and moral theory
      What this source supports

      A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

      Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    Possible inconsistency

    A retained protection and the plan’s known cost

    Your earlier answer either prohibits the stated breach of the needs floor or requires funded compensation before imposing it. The later answer proceeds with the same breach while the scenario explicitly withholds funded compensation. Whichever earlier protection you selected, it cannot govern this plan unchanged alongside that action.

    Scope: Only this plan, these households and this defined floor. No comparison is inferred between general concern for current people and general concern for future people. A deliberate revision can explain the difference.

    Exact selected-answer combination

    (“A proposed transition could protect the atmosphere while leaving some already poor households below a defined basic-needs floor. What principle governs this plan?” → “This plan must never knowingly push those households below that floor, even to secure its environmental gains.” or “Require a funded compensation arrangement before imposing the transition cost.”) AND (“Compensation cannot be funded before the deadline. Should this same plan proceed?” → “Proceed with this plan despite the known year below the same basic-needs floor.”)

    • Contractualism and UtilitarianismPrimary philosophy · In Utilitarianism and Beyond, pp. 103–128; indexed opening discussion of justification and moral theory
      What this source supports

      A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

      Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

      Limits: Not a full-book review. MORARG develops its own constructive proposal; Scanlon’s contractualism alone does not establish antirealism.

    • Montreal Protocol, Article 10: Financial mechanismPrimary treaty text · Current consolidated Article 10, especially paragraphs 1–3, 6 and 9
      What this source supports

      An actual mechanism for financial and technical cooperation, including a Multilateral Fund.

      Access: Official consolidated article inspected in pass002; 1990 denotes introduction of the mechanism, not the date of every later amendment

      Limits: The text establishes institutional arrangements. It does not prove their fairness, implementation success, or moral authority.

    Possible inconsistency

    Present participation and a present reciprocal claim

    The first answer makes present ability to answer necessary for a reciprocal claim. The second attributes a reciprocal claim now to people explicitly lacking that ability. These claims conflict under the same meaning of “reciprocal claim”; a welfare-based duty alone would not create this conflict.

    Scope: A narrow cross-room check about present reciprocal standing. It does not equate future welfare with reciprocity, require actual bargaining for every moral duty, or penalise uncertainty, realism or concern for non-speaking people.

    Exact selected-answer combination

    (“What gives someone who cannot currently answer a moral claim on the decision-makers?” → “A reciprocal claim requires present ability to answer; others may still be protected on welfare grounds.”) AND (“Do these future people already have reciprocal claims on the present decision-makers?” → “They have reciprocal claims now despite having no present ability to answer.”)

      What this source supports

      A primary route into interpersonal justification and its substantive moral background.

      Access: Selected indexed primary passages inspected in pass002; scanned PDF retrieved but text extraction was empty

      Access: Primary excerpts inspected in pass001; full article not newly reviewed in pass002

      Limits: This is a political account, not a complete antirealist theory of morality.

    Examine the sources

    The type, locator, access note and limits explain what each source can support.

    Good and Evil

    Peter T. Geach · 1956 · Primary philosophy: article transcription · geach-1956

    Argues that good and bad are logically attributive, and rejects reducing their descriptive use to the speaker’s commendation or tastes.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Analysis 17(2), 33–42; transcription paragraphs 1–3, 8–12 and 15–16
    Access: Full article transcription available; selected passages inspected in pass004; publisher-indexed title, DOI and pagination checked, while direct publisher retrieval failed
    Limits: Geach’s attributive thesis is contested. It does not entail subjectivism or MORARG’s constitutive thesis; his transition to moral assessment and motivation requires further argument. The transcription header gives 32–42; publisher metadata gives 33–42.

    Principia Ethica

    G. E. Moore · 1903 · Primary philosophy: public-domain transcription · moore-1903

    Distinguishes a verbal definition from inquiry into goodness and argues against treating a substantive account of the good as a result of word meaning.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Chapter I, §§5–10, especially §6 on verbal definition and substantive claims; printed pp. 5–10
    Access: Full book transcription available at Project Gutenberg; selected opening passages inspected in pass004, not the whole book
    Limits: Moore’s indefinability thesis is his philosophical position, not an established refutation of every naturalist identity theory. MORARG uses it as a serious countermodel to the proposed semantic shortcut.

    Authority and Second-Personal Reasons for Acting

    Stephen Darwall · 2009 · Primary philosophy: author manuscript · darwall-2009

    Distinguishes reasons for beneficial compliance from a person’s standing to demand compliance and hold another accountable.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Reasons for Action, chapter 7, pp. 134–154; accessed manuscript pp. 1–7 and 13–16
    Access: Yale-hosted full manuscript available; selected passages on authority, accountability and expertise inspected in pass004; Cambridge publication record verified
    Limits: The interdefinition of valid demand and authority does not establish everyone’s standing or prove antirealism. The 2006 book and 2013 reprint were not reviewed in full.

    The Justification of Basic Rights: A Discourse-Theoretical Approach

    Rainer Forst · 2016 · Primary philosophy: author manuscript · forst-2016

    Connects equal normative authority with reciprocal justification and explicitly states that constructed norms depend on prior normativity of principles and standing.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Netherlands Journal of Legal Philosophy 45(3), 7–28; manuscript §§1–2, pp. 1–9; especially p. 8
    Access: Berkeley-hosted full manuscript available; selected grounding passages inspected in pass004; publisher-indexed PDF verified publication metadata but direct publisher retrieval failed
    Limits: Focuses on basic legal rights of recognised members; the membership question is bracketed. Not a premise-free derivation, proof of all moral constructivism or independent review of every rival rights theory.

    The Right to Justification: Elements of a Constructivist Theory of Justice

    Rainer Forst; translated by Jeffrey Flynn · 2012 · Primary philosophy: selected book passages · forst-2012

    Locates moral responsibility in relation to other people while retaining a substantive duty of reciprocal justification that is not created by a promise or optional agreement.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Introduction, printed pp. 5–6; chapters 1–2, selected pp. 35–39 and 53–55
    Access: Full English book PDF available through an academic-hosted copy; selected passages inspected in pass004, not the full book; Columbia publication record checked
    Limits: The fundamental duty remains normative. MORARG draws on the relational argument without adopting every claim or treating a refuser as logically refuted.

    Being Realistic about Reasons: John Locke Lectures 4 and 5

    T. M. Scanlon · 2009 · Primary philosophy: lecture manuscripts · scanlon-2009

    Distinguishes construction of interpersonal right and wrong from prior reasons, and epistemic justification from normative grounding; Lecture 5 examines contextual comparisons of reasons.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Lecture 4, pp. 1–3, 13–20 and 24–26; Lecture 5, pp. 15–21
    Access: Official Oxford-hosted full lecture PDFs available; selected sections inspected in pass003
    Limits: These are 2009 lectures, not the revised 2014 book. Selected passages do not provide a complete theory of conflicting claims or settle the prior-reasons objection.

    Wrongness and Reasons: A Re-examination

    T. M. Scanlon · 2007 · Primary philosophy: chapter in publisher preview · scanlon-2007

    Reconsiders wrongness and reasons; treats contractualism as an account of an interpersonal form of wrongness rather than every possible moral ground.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Oxford Studies in Metaethics, volume 2, pp. 5–20; especially pp. 7–11 and 16–18
    Access: Full chapter present in publisher/distributor preview; selected passages inspected in pass003
    Limits: MORARG owns its stronger constitutive proposal. This preview includes only the opening page of the following Enoch chapter and does not establish that chapter was read.

    Agency, Shmagency: Why Normativity Won’t Come from What Is Constitutive of Action

    David Enoch · 2006 · Primary philosophy: journal text through public mirror · enoch-2006

    Constitutive features of agency do not by themselves establish their authority; the later epistemic appeal to deliberative indispensability is a separate argument.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: The Philosophical Review 115(2), 169–198; §3, pp. 177–180; discussion of inescapability; §§7–8, pp. 192–196
    Access: Selected primary text inspected through an indexed public mirror; title and publication checked against author records; publisher PDF not retrieved
    Limits: Mirror OCR can contain errors. This is a selected-argument review, not a full reading of Taking Morality Seriously or all replies to constitutivism.

    Can There Be a Global, Interesting, Coherent Constructivism about Practical Reason?

    David Enoch · 2009 · Primary philosophy: manuscript through public mirror · enoch-2009

    Distinguishes discovery procedures from constitutive ones, and local construction from a global account of practical reasons; challenges the latter’s deliberative resources.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Philosophical Explorations 12(3), 319–339; manuscript §§2.1–2.3, 4.1 and 5 (draft pp. 5–9, 16–18 and 27–36)
    Access: Selected sections of a full indexed primary manuscript inspected; journal metadata and DOI verified at Hebrew University
    Limits: The accessed text is a draft, not a line-by-line comparison with the final publication. A local interpersonal account avoids the global target only by openly defending its remaining normative premises.

    Kantian Constructivism in Moral Theory

    John Rawls · 1980 · Primary philosophy: journal text through public mirror · rawls-1980

    Person-conceptions and reasonable constraints shape a construction; the reasonable is not reduced to individual advantage.

    Locator, access and limits

    Access: Selected primary-text OCR passages inspected in pass003; JSTOR publication record checked
    Limits: Rawls frames justice as fairness for democratic public culture. This is not a general proof of MORARG’s metaethics, nor a complete review of Rawls’s later political liberalism.

    Limits: The interpretation as inequity aversion is contested by later reanalysis and alternative explanations.

    No evidence for inequity aversion in non-human animals: a meta-analysis of accept/reject paradigms

    Oded Ritov, Christoph J. Völter, Nichola J. Raihani and Jan M. Engelmann · 2024 · Primary empirical research · ritov-2024

    Reports no statistically clear pooled evidence of inequity aversion in studied accept/reject tasks.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Proceedings of the Royal Society B 291, 20241452; individual-participant data, 23 studies and 18 species
    Access: Indexed primary full text and abstract inspected; PMC page returned an access challenge on this pass
    Limits: A null result is not proof of no effect or no capacity in all paradigms; samples and available data limit inference.

    Capuchin monkeys’ responses to inequity in a group context

    Mayte Martínez and Sarah F. Brosnan · 2025 · Primary empirical research · martinez-capuchin-2025

    Reports refusals of low-value rewards under disadvantageous inequity, with separate contrast and advantageous-inequity responses in a group setting.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Animal Behaviour 226, 123257; abstract and indexed discussion excerpts
    Access: Publisher abstract and selected indexed snippets inspected; full paper not reviewed
    Limits: Food value and contrast affect interpretation; number of trades did not depend on the partner’s reward. No independent reanalysis.

    Chimpanzees’ responses to inequity in a group context are influenced by food quality and their relationships with the group members present

    Mayte Martínez and colleagues · 2026 · Primary empirical research · martinez-chimp-2026

    Reports context-sensitive responses in a group of chimpanzees.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Proceedings of the Royal Society B 293, 20260397; selected §§2–4, design, measures and discussion
    Access: Pass003: selected methods, results and discussion inspected in publisher-provided full text hosted on ResearchGate; no data reanalysis
    Limits: Voluntary participation in six captive groups; affiliation is not experimentally assigned. Advantageous refusals were too rare for statistical analysis. These measures do not establish a justice concept.

    No Response to Inconsistently Experienced Inequity in Four Primate Species

    Simmons and colleagues · 2026 · Primary empirical research · simmons-2026

    At 25% or 50% unequal trials the studied primates did not increase refusals relative to controls.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: American Journal of Primatology; DOI 10.1002/ajp.70196; abstract
    Access: Publisher and PubMed primary-study abstracts inspected; full text not reviewed
    Limits: Null refusal effects do not establish absence of noticing or other responses; task frequency and measures matter.

    Infants’ Social Evaluation of Helpers and Hinderers: A Large-Scale, Multi-Lab, Coordinated Replication Study

    Kelsey Lucca, Francis Yuen, Yiyi Wang and colleagues · 2025 · Primary empirical research · lucca-2025

    A large coordinated hill-task replication found no above-chance helper preference.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Developmental Science 28, e13581; abstract; correction DOI 10.1111/desc.70029
    Access: Primary abstract and indexed methods inspected; published correction inspected by Terra; no independent reanalysis
    Limits: 567 included infants aged 5.5–10.5 months; this task is not a test of all moral capacities. The 2025 correction changes an exploratory analysis, with conclusions unchanged.

    Infants use helping to infer the existence and strength of caring relationships

    Bill Pepe, Brandon Woo, Ashley J. Thomas and Lindsey J. Powell · 2026 · Primary empirical research · pepe-2026

    Looking-time findings in 14–15-month-olds suggest relationship-specific expectations about helping.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: PNAS 123, e2531771123; abstract, four preregistered experiments
    Access: Primary abstract and selected indexed methods/results inspected; full independent assessment not completed
    Limits: An indirect measure at a different age, not a direct replication of the hill-choice task; it establishes neither innateness nor moral authority.

    Bonobos respond aversively to unequal reward distributions

    Kia Radovanović and colleagues · 2025 · Primary empirical research · radovanovic-2025

    Two bonobo studies report unequal-reward responses; a human/machine manipulation challenges the social-disappointment explanation.

    Locator, access and limits

    Locator: Proceedings of the Royal Society B 292, 20242873; §§2–4, subjects, controls, results and limitations
    Access: Primary full-text indexed methods/results/discussion inspected in pass003; no independent reanalysis
    Limits: Six newly tested bonobos in one group; one analysis pooled an older sample. Study 2 was only partly consistent with inequity aversion; partner variation and task constraints limit generalisation.

    About the argument

    The experience advocates an intersubjective account with personal decision-making. Its arguments are authored, its primary sources can be examined, and its normative commitments are open to challenge. The separate 3D moral mechanism is outside this project.

    Rooms and diagrams are symbolic settings. Historical and empirical case facts are labelled and sourced; scenarios and proposed decisions are distinguished from those facts. Original transplant imagery is preserved in the package, while the active walls carry topical argument diagrams.

    Responses, collected values, working reasons and consistency explanations stay in page memory and clear on reload. You may download a private JSON record with epoch-millisecond timestamps. Nothing is submitted; there are no accounts or analytics.

    MORARG · Morality, Between UsArgument edition 0.5.1
